Bose-Einstein condensation of interacting bosons: A two-step proof
András Sütő

Abstract
We prove two equilibrium properties of a system of interacting atoms in three or higher dimensional continuous space. (i) If the particles interact via pair potentials of a nonnegative Fourier transform, their self-organization into infinite permutation cycles is simultaneous with off-diagonal long-range order. If the cycle lengths tend to infinity not slower than the square of the linear extension of the system, there is also Bose-Einstein condensation. (ii) If the pair potential is also nonnegative, cycles composed of a nonzero fraction of the total number of particles do appear if the density exceeds a temperature-dependent threshold value. The two together constitute the proof that in such a system Bose-Einstein condensation takes place at high enough densities. Contents 1. Introduction 2. Two-step proof for the ideal Bose gas 3. Remarks to Lemma 1.1 4. Proof of Lemma 1.1 4.1 Discrete-time approximation 4.2 Fourier expansions 4.3 The case x ≠ 0 x\neq 0 4.4 The limit of continuous time 4.5 Merger graphs: Analysis of the constraint 5. Proof of Theorem 1.1 5.1 Condition for ODLRO 5.2 Condition for BEC 6. Proof of Lemma 1.2 7. Proof of Theorem 1.2 7.1 Cycle-decoupling model 7.2 Coupling of the cycles 8. Physical meaning of the permutation cycles 9. Historical notes



1
Introduction

The aim of this paper is to prove Bose-Einstein condensation (BEC) in a system of particles interacting via pair potentials that are both positive and of the positive type.
The main example is a Gaussian function whose Fourier transform is also a Gaussian.
More generally, pair potentials that are both positive and positive-type result as the autocorrelation function of some integrable nonnegative function
v
,

u(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}v(x+y)v(y){\,\rm d}y,

(1.1)

in which case
\hat{u}(z)=|\hat{v}(z)|^{2}
.
Our guiding idea is to separate the proof into two steps based on the seminal works of Feynman [F2] and of Oliver Penrose and Onsager [Pen].

In the early days of the research on BEC for interacting bosons a main concern was how to infer from the formulas
\langle N_{0}\rangle
, the expected number of particles in the zero-momentum plane-wave state, if one does not use the second-quantized formalism. The answer was given by Oliver Penrose and Onsager in 1956 who established a connection between
\langle N_{0}\rangle
and the largest eigenvalue of the one-particle reduced density matrix. We present their conclusion for particles in the canonical ensemble on the cube

\Lambda=(-L/2,L/2]^{d}
with periodic boundary conditions, when the connection appears in its clearest form.
For
N
particles of mass
m_{0}
on the torus
\Lambda
the Hamiltonian is

H_{N,L}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Delta_{i}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}u_{L}(x_{j}-x_{i}).

(1.2)

Working on the torus we must periodize the pair potential
u
which gives

u_{L}(x)=\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}u(x+Lz).

(1.3)

The partition function for this system is

Q_{N,L}={\rm\,Tr\,}P_{+}e^{-\beta H_{N,L}},\qquad P_{+}=\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{N}}P_{\pi}.

(1.4)

P_{+}
is the orthogonal projection to the symmetric subspace,
S_{N}
is the symmetric group and
P_{\pi}
are permutation matrices. The one-body reduced density matrix is

\sigma^{N,L}_{1}=\frac{N}{Q_{N,L}}{\rm\,Tr\,}_{2,\dots,N}P_{+}e^{-\beta H_{N,L}}

(1.5)

where
{\rm\,Tr\,}_{2,\dots,N}
is the partial trace over
N-1
particles. In the first-quantized formalism the number operator for particles in the plane-wave state
L^{-d/2}e^{{\,\rm i}k\cdot x}
is

N_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}N_{k,j},\quad N_{k,j}=I_{j-1}\otimes|k\rangle\langle k|\otimes I_{N-j}.

(1.6)

Here
|k\rangle\langle k|
is the orthogonal projection to the one-dimensional subspace spanned by
L^{-d/2}e^{{\,\rm i}k\cdot x}
and
I_{j-1}
and
I_{N-j}
are identity operators in the
j-1
- and
N-j
-particle Hilbert spaces, respectively. It is not difficult to show [Su6] that

\sigma^{N,L}_{1}=\sum_{k\in(2\pi/L){\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\langle N_{k}\rangle|k\rangle\langle k|

(1.7)

where

\langle N_{k}\rangle=Q_{N,L}^{-1}{\rm\,Tr\,}P_{+}e^{-\beta H_{N,L}}N_{k}.

(1.8)

Let
\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|y\rangle
denote the integral kernel of
\sigma^{N,L}_{1}
, it then follows that

\langle N_{0}\rangle=\int_{\Lambda}\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle{\,\rm d}x.

(1.9)

Feynman in 1953 applied his path-integral method (the Feynman-Kac formula) to analyze the superfluid transition. His conclusion was that the superfluid transition must be accompanied by the appearance of ”long” permutation cycles. To combine the result of Penrose and Onsager with the idea of Feynman we need the path-integral form of
Q_{N,L}
and
\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|y\rangle
.

Let
P^{t}_{xy}({\,\rm d}\omega)
be the Wiener measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
for trajectories
\omega
that start in
x
at time 0 and end in
y
at time
t
.
By definition, the analogous measure on the torus of side
L
is

W^{t}_{xy}({\,\rm d}\omega)=\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}P^{t}_{x,y+Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega).

(1.10)

The Feynman-Kac form of the partition function reads

Q_{N,L}=\sum_{p=1}^{N}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\geq 1:\sum n_{l}=N}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\frac{1}{n_{l}}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x_{l}\int W_{x_{l}x_{l}}^{n_{l}\beta}({\,\rm d}\omega_{l})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l})}\left(\prod_{1\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l^{\prime}},\omega_{l})}\right)\,.

(1.11)

Because

\sum_{p=1}^{N}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\geq 1:\sum n_{l}=N}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\frac{1}{n_{l}}=1,

(1.12)

this is an average over the conjugation classes of
S_{N}
that replaces the average over
S_{N}
.
The functionals
U(\omega_{l})
and
U(\omega_{l^{\prime}},\omega_{l})
represent the potential energy:

U(\omega_{l})=\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{\beta}\sum_{0\leq j<k\leq n_{l}-1}u_{L}(\omega_{l}(k\beta+t)-\omega_{l}(j\beta+t)){\,\rm d}t

(1.13)

is the potential energy of the
n_{l}
particles forming the trajectory
\omega_{l}
, and

U(\omega_{l^{\prime}},\omega_{l})=\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{\beta}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}\sum_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}u_{L}(\omega_{l}(k\beta+t)-\omega_{l^{\prime}}(j\beta+t)){\,\rm d}t

(1.14)

is the energy of interaction between the trajectories
\omega_{l}
and
\omega_{l^{\prime}}
.
Distinguishing the trajectory that contains particle no.1,
Q_{N,L}
can be rewritten in a form which is almost a recurrence relation (it
is
for the noninteracting gas),

Q_{N,L}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\int W^{n\beta}_{xx}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{0})}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0}).

(1.15)

Here
Q_{0,L}(\omega_{0})=1
, and for
n<N

\displaystyle Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0})=\sum_{p=1}^{N-n}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\geq 1:\sum n_{l}=N-n}\,\prod_{l=1}^{p}\frac{1}{n_{l}}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x_{l}\int W_{x_{l}x_{l}}^{n_{l}\beta}({\,\rm d}\omega_{l})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l})}

\displaystyle\times\left(\prod_{1\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l^{\prime}},\omega_{l})}\right)\,\,e^{-\beta\sum_{l=1}^{p}U(\omega_{0},\omega_{l})}.

(1.16)

Without
e^{-\beta\sum_{l=1}^{p}U(\omega_{0},\omega_{l})}
this is just
Q_{N-n,L}
.

The path-integral form of
\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle
is

\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle=Q_{N,L}^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int W^{n\beta}_{0x}({\,\rm d}\omega)e^{-\beta U(\omega)}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega).

(1.17)

It shows that not only
\sigma^{N,L}_{1}
is a positive operator but
\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|y\rangle
is a ”matrix” of positive elements. The Banach-space analogue of the Perron-Frobenius theorem applies,
\langle N_{0}\rangle
is the largest eigenvalue with eigenvector identically equalling
L^{-d/2}
.
The condensate density is

\rho_{0}^{N,L}=\frac{\langle N_{0}\rangle}{L^{d}}=\frac{1}{L^{d}}\int_{\Lambda}\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle{\,\rm d}x=\frac{\rho}{NQ{{}_{N,L}}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\int W^{n\beta}_{0x}({\,\rm d}\omega)e^{-\beta U(\omega)}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega)

(1.18)

where
\rho=N/L^{d}
, the total density. Note that
\omega
in
\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle
is an
open
trajectory associated with a permutation
cycle
, just as the closed trajectories
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{p}
do; all are effective single-particle trajectories. Further on we use the words trajectory and cycle as synonyms.

We need the density (number per unit volume)
\rho^{N,L}_{n}
of particles in permutation cycles of length
n
, where the length is the number of particles in the cycle. Because the particles are indistinguishable,
\rho_{n}^{N,L}
equals
\rho
times the probability according to the canonical Gibbs distribution that particle no.1 is in a cycle of length
n
. This latter is

\frac{1}{NQ_{N,L}}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\int W^{n\beta}_{xx}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{0})}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0}),

so using translation invariance

\rho^{N,L}_{n}=\frac{\rho}{NQ_{N,L}}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\int W^{n\beta}_{xx}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{0})}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0})=\frac{1}{Q_{N,L}}\int W^{n\beta}_{00}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{0})}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0}).

(1.19)

Inserting this expression into the formulas for
\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle
and
\rho^{N,L}_{0}
,

\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\,\frac{\int W^{n\beta}_{0x}({\,\rm d}\omega)e^{-\beta U(\omega)}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega)}{\int W^{n\beta}_{00}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{0})}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0})},

(1.20)

\rho^{N,L}_{0}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\,\frac{\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\int W^{n\beta}_{0x}({\,\rm d}\omega)e^{-\beta U(\omega)}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega)}{L^{d}\int W^{n\beta}_{00}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{0})}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0})}.

(1.21)

Because
\rho=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}
, with
\rho_{n}=\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\rho^{N,L}_{n}

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{M}\rho^{N,L}_{n}+\sum_{M+1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\right]

(1.22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}+\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{M+1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\,\geq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}.

(1.23)

The infinite sum is the density of particles in finite cycles in the infinite system. If the inequality is strict, there are also infinite cycles in the infinite system.

Now we are able to state the two steps mentioned in the title.

(i) Prove that if (and only if)
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}<\rho

and
the diverging cycle lengths tend to infinity sufficiently fast, there is BEC.

(ii) Prove that for a class of pair interactions at sufficiently high densities
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}<\rho
does occur and the diverging cycle lengths tend to infinity fast enough.

Infinite cycles, off-diagonal long-range order (ODLRO) and BEC are three distinct notions. By definition, BEC implies ODLRO which implies infinite cycles, but implication in the opposite direction is subject to conditions.
Both ODLRO and BEC are related to the infinite-volume limit of
\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle
. There is ODLRO if

\lim_{x\to\infty}\,\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle\neq 0,

including the possibility that the limit does not exist, and there is BEC if

\rho_{0}=\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\,\rho^{N,L}_{0}=\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\frac{1}{L^{d}}\int_{\Lambda}\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle{\,\rm d}x>0.

Thus, in principle, ODLRO can exist without BEC, but the opposite is obviously false. Also, infinite cycles do not necessarily mean ODLRO. It will be seen that for positive-type pair potentials the existence of infinite cycles suffices for ODLRO, but for BEC there must be cycles of length diverging at least as fast as
N^{2/d}
.
The significance of
n\propto N^{2/d}\propto L^{2}/\lambda_{\beta}^{2}
is clear: this is the order of magnitude of the necessary number of steps for a random walk of step length
\lambda_{\beta}
that starts from zero to attain any point of a cube of side
L
.

Before going further we introduce three more notations.

–

\lambda_{\beta}=\sqrt{2\pi\hbar^{2}\beta/m_{0}}
the thermal wave length,

–

\sigma_{1}(x)=\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\,\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle
,

–

\hat{u}
the Fourier transform of
u
.

The first step of the proof is

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider
N
identical bosons on a
d\geq 3
-torus of side
L
at inverse temperature
\beta
that interact via a pair potential
u:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(i)

u
is positive-type (
\hat{u}\geq 0
) and
\int\hat{u}(x)x^{2}{\,\rm d}x<\infty
,

(ii)

u(x)=O\left(|x|^{-\eta}\right)
with some
\eta>d
as
x\to\infty
(condition for periodization).

For this system the following hold true:

1.

\rho-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}\ \leq\ \sigma_{1}(x)\ \leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}+\rho-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}

(1.24)

implying

\lim_{x\to\infty}\sigma_{1}(x)=\rho-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}.

(1.25)

2.

For any
c>0

\rho^{\infty}_{c}\ \leq\ \rho_{0}\ \leq\ \lim_{c^{\prime}\downarrow 0}\rho^{\infty}_{c^{\prime}}.

(1.26)

Here

\rho^{\infty}_{c}=\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{n=\lfloor cN^{2/d}\rfloor}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{\nu^{N,L}_{n}(y)\,{\,\rm d}y}{\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}z\cdot\left(z+2Ly\right)\right\}}.

(1.27)

\int\nu^{N,L}_{n}{\,\rm d}y=1
,
\nu^{N,L}_{n}
is concentrated to an
O(1/\sqrt{n})
-neighborhood of the origin, making sure that if

\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{n=\lfloor cN^{2/d}\rfloor}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}>0

(1.28)

then
\rho_{0}>0
. If the infinite cycles are exclusively macroscopic then

\rho_{0}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\,\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{n=\lfloor\varepsilon N\rfloor}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}.

(1.29)

Concerning ODLRO, the theorem is about the analysis of the fraction within the sum (
1.20
) when
x
is fixed, and
N
and
n
go to infinity. It will be seen that for any sequence
g_{N}\to\infty
and
n\geq g_{N}
the fraction tends to one so it does not prevent cycles of diverging lengths contributing to the sum. Note the (most probably monotonic) decay of
\sigma_{1}(x)
from
\sigma_{1}(0)=\rho
to
\sigma_{1}(\infty)=\rho-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}
.

The bounds in (
1.26
) show that a sufficient and necessary condition for BEC is the existence of cycles whose length diverges at least as fast as
N^{2/d}
.
If
n\geq cN^{2/d}
then
1/\sqrt{n}\leq 1/(\sqrt{c}\rho^{1/d}L)
. Therefore,

L|y|=O(1)
in the domain of concentration of
\nu^{N,L}_{n}(y)
, and
the sum over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
in (
1.27
) remains bounded as
N,L\to\infty
. Within the terms belonging to
n\propto N
,
L|y|=O(N^{1/d-1/2})
, and the whole integral tends to 1, which explains Eq. (
1.29
).

Let

\displaystyle G\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x)=\prod_{l=1}^{p}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x_{l}\int W_{x_{l}x_{l}}^{n_{l}\beta}({\,\rm d}\omega_{l})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l})}\left(\prod_{1\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l^{\prime}},\omega_{l})}\right)

\displaystyle L^{d}\int W_{0x}^{n\beta}({\,\rm d}\omega)e^{-\beta U(\omega)}\,e^{-\beta\sum_{l=1}^{p}U(\omega,\omega_{l})}

(1.30)

and for
n<N

G^{N}_{n}(x)=\sum_{p=1}^{N-n}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\geq 1:\sum n_{l}=N-n}\,\frac{1}{\prod_{l=1}^{p}n_{l}}G\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x)=L^{d}\int W_{0x}^{n\beta}({\,\rm d}\omega)e^{-\beta U(\omega)}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega).

(1.31)

Setting
Q_{0,L}(\omega)=1
, the right-hand side of this equation defines also
G^{N}_{N}(x)
. With the help of

G^{N}_{n}(x)
the equations (
1.20
) and (
1.21
) take on the form

\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\,\frac{G^{N}_{n}(x)}{G^{N}_{n}(0)},\qquad\rho^{N,L}_{0}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\,\frac{\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\,G^{N}_{n}(x)}{L^{d}\,G^{N}_{n}(0)}.

(1.32)

Also, with

G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]=\prod_{l=1}^{p}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x_{l}\int W_{x_{l}x_{l}}^{n_{l}\beta}({\,\rm d}\omega_{l})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l})}\left(\prod_{1\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l^{\prime}},\omega_{l})}\right)

(1.33)

and (
n_{0}=n
)

\displaystyle G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{0}^{p}\right]\equiv G\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]\equiv G\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](0)=\prod_{l=0}^{p}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x_{l}\int W_{x_{l}x_{l}}^{n_{l}\beta}({\,\rm d}\omega_{l})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l})}\left(\prod_{0\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l^{\prime}},\omega_{l})}\right)

\displaystyle=L^{d}\int W_{00}^{n_{0}\beta}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{0})}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x_{l}\int W_{x_{l}x_{l}}^{n_{l}\beta}({\,\rm d}\omega_{l})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l})}\left(\prod_{0\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}e^{-\beta U(\omega_{l^{\prime}},\omega_{l})}\right)

(1.34)

the partition function still can be written as

Q_{N,L}=\sum_{p=1}^{N}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\geq 1:\sum n_{l}=N}\frac{1}{\prod_{l=1}^{p}n_{l}}\ G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]

(1.35)

or, setting
G^{N}_{n}(0)=G^{N}_{n}
,

Q_{N,L}=\frac{1}{N}G[N]+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\sum_{p=1}^{N-n}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\geq 1:\sum n_{l}=N-n}\,\frac{1}{\prod_{l=1}^{p}n_{l}}G\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}G^{N}_{n},

(1.36)

the respective analogues of Eqs. (
1.11
) and (
1.15
). Comparison of Eqs. (
1
) and (
1
) shows that
G\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x)
is continuous at
x=0
. This is a consequence of translation invariance.

Throughout the paper the numbering of the cycles may run from 1 to
p
or from 0 to
p
if cycle 0 is to be distinguished. In the latter case we understand
n_{0}=n
, and thus
G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{0}^{p}\right](x)=G\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x)
.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
relies on the following lemma.

Lemma 1.1
.

Suppose that
\hat{u}
exists and is integrable. Then

\displaystyle G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{0}^{p}\right](x)=\exp\left\{-\frac{\beta\hat{u}(0)N(N-1)}{2L^{d}}\right\}\sum_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}|1\leq j<k\leq N\}}\ \prod_{1\leq j<k\leq N}\frac{1}{\alpha^{k}_{j}!}

(1.37)

\displaystyle\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}\frac{-\beta}{L^{d}}\sum_{z^{k}_{j,r}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\hat{u}\left(\frac{z^{k}_{j,r}}{L}\right)\int_{0}^{1}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,r}\ \left[\delta_{Z^{0}_{1},0}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\sum_{q=1}^{n_{0}-1}\int_{0}^{1}\left[z+Z_{q}(t)\right]^{2}{\,\rm d}t\right\}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{L}z\cdot x\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\prod_{l=1}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\int_{0}^{1}\left[z+Z_{q}(t)\right]^{2}{\,\rm d}t\right\}\right].

There is a summation with respect to
\alpha^{k}_{j}
for every pair
1\leq j<k\leq N
, and the quantity in the square brackets is under all these summations and integrals. Furthermore,

C_{l}=\{N_{l-1}+1,N_{l-1}+2,\dots,N_{l}\}\quad\mbox{where}\quad N_{l}=\sum_{l^{\prime}=0}^{l}n_{l^{\prime}}\quad(N_{p}=N)

(1.38)

and for
q\in C_{l}

\displaystyle Z_{q}(t)=-\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}\sum_{k=q}^{N_{l}}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\bf 1}_{\{t^{k}_{j,r}\geq t\}}z^{k}_{j,r}+\sum_{j=q}^{N_{l}}\sum_{k=N_{l}+1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\bf 1}_{\{t^{k}_{j,r}\geq t\}}z^{k}_{j,r}

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=q+1}^{N_{l}}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\bf 1}_{\{t^{k}_{j,r}<t\}}z^{k}_{j,r}+\sum_{j=q+1}^{N_{l}}\sum_{k=N_{l}+1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\bf 1}_{\{t^{k}_{j,r}<t\}}z^{k}_{j,r}\ .

(1.39)

In particular,

Z^{l}_{1}:=Z_{N_{l-1}+1}(0)=-\sum_{j=1}^{N_{l-1}}\sum_{k\in C_{l}}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}z^{k}_{j,r}+\sum_{j\in C_{l}}\sum_{k=N_{l}+1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}z^{k}_{j,r}\ .

(1.40)

Preceding the proof of this lemma complementary information will be given in Section 3.

The second step of the proof is done in

Theorem 1.2
.

Consider
N
identical bosons on a
d\geq 3
-torus of side
L
at inverse temperature
\beta
that interact via a pair potential
u:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(i)

u\geq 0
and
\hat{u}\geq 0
,

(ii)

u(x)=O\left(|x|^{-\eta}\right)
with some
\eta>d
as
x\to\infty
,
\hat{u}
is integrable and
\int\hat{u}(x)x^{2}{\,\rm d}x<\infty
.

There exists a temperature-dependent positive number
\zeta_{c}(\beta)
such that if
\rho>\zeta_{c}(\beta)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}
then

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}=\frac{\zeta_{c}(\beta)}{\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\rho-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\,\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\,\sum_{n\geq\varepsilon N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}=\rho_{0}>0.

(1.41)

The inequality
\rho_{0}>0
is already the corollary of the two theorems.
Theorem
1.2
is also based on Lemma
1.1
, but a key element of the proof is another lemma. Below
\zeta
is Riemann’s zeta function.

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
F_{N,L}
denote the free energy and

f(\rho,\beta)=\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}L^{-d}F_{N,L}=-\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\ \frac{1}{\beta L^{d}}\ln Q_{N,L}

(1.42)

the free energy density for an integrable superstable pair potential
u
[R2, R3], and let
F^{0}_{N,L}
and
f^{0}(\rho,\beta)
be the same quantities for the ideal Bose gas. Then

\frac{C_{\Lambda}[u]}{L^{d}}N(N-1)-BN+F^{0}_{N,L}\leq F_{N,L}\leq\frac{\|u\|_{1}}{2L^{d}}N(N-1)+\frac{2^{d/2-1}\zeta(d/2)\|u\|_{1}}{\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}N+F^{0}_{N,L}

(1.43)

and

C[u]\rho^{2}-B\rho+f^{0}(\rho,\beta)\leq f(\rho,\beta)\leq\frac{\|u\|_{1}}{2}\rho^{2}+\frac{2^{d/2-1}\zeta(d/2)\|u\|_{1}}{\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\rho+f^{0}(\rho,\beta)

(1.44)

where
B>0
and
0<C_{\Lambda}[u],C[u]\leq\hat{u}(0)/2
.
If
u
is bounded, for
B
one can substitute
u_{L}(0)/2
in the first line and
u(0)/2
in the second line. For a positive-type
u
,
C_{\Lambda}[u]=C[u]=\hat{u}(0)/2
. Thus, if both
u\geq 0
and
\hat{u}\geq 0
then

\frac{\hat{u}(0)}{2}\rho^{2}-\frac{u(0)}{2}\rho+f^{0}(\rho,\beta)\leq f(\rho,\beta)\leq\frac{\hat{u}(0)}{2}\rho^{2}+\frac{2^{d/2-1}\zeta(d/2)\hat{u}(0)}{\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\rho+f^{0}(\rho,\beta).

(1.45)

The bounds (
1.45
) explain why we are able to show (
1.41
) if both
u
and
\hat{u}
are nonnegative. By
subtracting the mean-field contribution
\hat{u}(0)N(N-1)/2L^{d}
from the potential energy the interaction loses its superstability but remains stable; meanwhile, the condition for BEC is unchanged. In this regard the system becomes similar to the noninteracting gas, and comparison with it facilitates the proof. The result is also similar.

A more fundamental reason why
u\geq 0
and
\hat{u}\geq 0
can help the proof of (
1.41
) is as follows.
If
\hat{u}\geq 0
, at high densities the distribution of particles in classical ground states is uniform, while if
\hat{u}
has a negative part, there is some structural order [Su5]. Also, at moderate densities the ground state can be highly degenerate for
\hat{u}\geq 0
, as it was shown for
\hat{u}
having a compact support [Su13]. The uniform distribution in the ground state most probably excludes any stable crystalline phase at positive temperatures. Moreover,
u\geq 0
excludes classical condensation. On the contrary, if
u
or
\hat{u}
has a negative part, classical condensation or crystallization may take place at a higher temperature than that of the expected appearance of infinite cycles, so the latter should be proven in a restricted ensemble: in a liquid (the liquid helium) or in a crystal [Uel1].

It is worth noting how the conditions on the pair potential strengthen as we advance in the proof. For Lemma
1.1
only the existence and integrability of
\hat{u}
is necessary. For Theorem
1.1
we need
\hat{u}\geq 0
and
\int\hat{u}(x)x^{2}{\,\rm d}x<\infty
, and for Theorem
1.2
we have to ask
u\geq 0
as well.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 we present the two-step proof for the ideal Bose gas. This result is not new, it appeared in papers [Su1] and [Su2], but the proof is new and its analogue will be applicable to the interacting gas.
Sections 3, 4 and 5 contain some explaining remarks about Lemma
1.1
, the proof of the lemma and the proof of Theorem
1.1
, respectively. In a first reading Section 4 can be skipped.
Sections 6 and 7 are devoted to the proof of Lemma
1.2
and Theorem
1.2
.
The physical meaning of the permutation cycles is not obvious, a possible interpretation is given in Section 8. Section 9 ends the article with a survey of the long history of the research on BEC.

2
Two-step proof for the ideal Bose gas

If there is no interaction,
G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{0}^{p}\right]
becomes

G^{0}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{0}^{p}\right]=\prod_{l=0}^{p}q_{n_{l}}

(2.1)

where

q_{n}=\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}z^{2}\right\}=\frac{L^{d}}{n^{d/2}\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi L^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}z^{2}\right\},

(2.2)

the one-particle partition function at inverse temperature
n\beta
, a monotone decreasing function of
n
bounded below by 1. Thus, the canonical partition function on the
d
-torus of side
L
is

Q^{0}_{N,L}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}q_{n}Q^{0}_{N-n,L}.

(2.3)

Together with the initial condition
Q^{0}_{0,L}=1
, Eq. (
2.3
) defines recursively
Q^{0}_{N,L}
. From this equation, which could be obtained without referring to
G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{0}^{p}\right]
, one can easily reproduce most of the results about cycle percolation (the appearance of infinite permutation cycles) and its connection with BEC, obtained in [Su1, 2].

With the help of the single-particle energies
\epsilon_{k}=\hbar^{2}k^{2}/2m_{0}
,
k\in(2\pi/L){\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
, the canonical partition function can still be written as

Q^{0}_{N,L}=\sum_{\sum_{k\neq 0}n_{k}\leq N}e^{-\beta\sum n_{k}\epsilon_{k}}=\sum_{M=0}^{N}\widehat{Q}^{0}_{M},

(2.4)

where

\widehat{Q}^{0}_{M}=\sum_{\sum_{k\neq 0}n_{k}=M}e^{-\beta\sum n_{k}\epsilon_{k}},

(2.5)

and the summations run over sets
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in(2\pi/L){\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}
of nonnegative integers. This shows that
Q^{0}_{N,L}>Q^{0}_{N-1,L}
, a crucial property for the proof of cycle percolation [Su1], which can also be obtained from (
2.3
).

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
A_{-1}=0
,
A_{0}=1
,

a_{1},a_{2},\ldots
arbitrary numbers, and for
N\geq 1
define recursively
A_{N}
by

A_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}a_{n}A_{N-n}.

(2.6)

Then

A_{N}-A_{N-1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}(a_{n}-1)(A_{N-n}-A_{N-n-1}).

(2.7)

Proof.
This follows by a simple computation.
\quad\Box

Now if
a_{n}>1
, then
A_{N}>A_{N-1}
can be proved by induction; and, because
q_{n}>1
, this applies to
Q_{N,L}^{0}
. Writing Eq. (
2.7
) for

Q^{0}_{N,L}-Q^{0}_{N-1,L}=\widehat{Q}^{0}_{N}
, keeping only the
n=1
term and iterating one obtains

\widehat{Q}^{0}_{N}>\frac{1}{N}(q_{1}-1)\widehat{Q}^{0}_{N-1}>\cdots>\frac{(q_{1}-1)^{N}}{N!}\quad(N\geq 1).

(2.8)

With
\widehat{Q}^{0}_{0}=1
one then concludes that for fixed
L
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}Q^{0}_{N,L}>e^{q_{1}-1}.

(2.9)

The limit is finite and is easy to compute from the middle member of (
2.4
). Because the restriction
\sum_{k\neq 0}n_{k}\leq N
drops, the multiple sum factorizes. Using
\beta\epsilon_{k}=\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}z^{2}/L^{2}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}Q^{0}_{N,L}=\prod_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\left[1-e^{-\pi(\lambda_{\beta}/L)^{2}z^{2}}\right]^{-1}=\exp\left\{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q_{n}-1}{n}\right\}\sim e^{\zeta(1+d/2)(L/\lambda_{\beta})^{d}}\quad(L/\lambda_{\beta}\gg 1).

(2.10)

Here
\zeta(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-x}
. The multiplier of
L^{d}
in the exponent is
-\beta
times the infinite-volume free energy density for the density
\rho
above its critical value,

f^{0}(\rho,\beta)=-\frac{\zeta(1+d/2)}{\beta\lambda_{\beta}^{d}},\quad\rho\geq\rho^{0}_{c}(\beta)=\frac{\zeta(d/2)}{\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}.

(2.11)

Some simple properties of the Wiener measure are useful:

\int P^{\beta}_{xy}({\,\rm d}\omega)=\lambda_{\beta}^{-d}e^{-\pi(y-x)^{2}/\lambda_{\beta}^{2}},\quad\int W^{n\beta}_{0x}({\,\rm d}\omega)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n\beta}^{d}}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}e^{-\pi(x+Lz)^{2}/\lambda_{n\beta}^{2}},

(2.12)

\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\int W^{n\beta}_{xx}({\,\rm d}\omega)=L^{d}\int W^{n\beta}_{00}({\,\rm d}\omega)=\frac{L^{d}}{\lambda_{n\beta}^{d}}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}e^{-\pi L^{2}z^{2}/\lambda_{n\beta}^{2}}=q_{n}

(2.13)

and

\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\int W^{n\beta}_{0x}({\,\rm d}\omega)=\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\int P^{\beta}_{0,x+Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\,\rm d}x\int P^{\beta}_{0,x}({\,\rm d}\omega)=1.

(2.14)

With them

\frac{\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\,G^{N}_{n}(x)}{L^{d}G^{N}_{n}(0)}=\frac{1}{q_{n}}.

(2.15)

The first step of the proof of BEC is as follows.

Theorem 2.1
.

In the ideal Bose gas there is BEC if and only if there exists a
c>0
such that

\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{n>cN^{2/d}}\rho^{N,L}_{n}>0.

(2.16)

Proof.
(i) Suppose first that (
2.16
) holds true. From Eqs. (
1.32
) and (
2.15
)

\rho^{N,L}_{0}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{\rho^{N,L}_{n}}{q_{n}}\geq\sum_{n>cN^{2/d}}\frac{\rho^{N,L}_{n}}{q_{n}}>\frac{\sum_{n>cN^{2/d}}\rho^{N,L}_{n}}{\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\{-\pi c(\rho^{1/d}\lambda_{\beta})^{2}z^{2}\}},

(2.17)

where we used the monotonic decrease of
q_{n}
. Taking the limit we find
\rho_{0}>0
.

(ii) Suppose now that for any
c>0
,

\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{n>cN^{2/d}}\rho^{N,L}_{n}=0.

We have

\displaystyle\rho^{N,L}_{0}=\sum_{n\leq cN^{2/d}}\frac{\rho^{N,L}_{n}}{q_{n}}+\sum_{n>cN^{2/d}}\frac{\rho^{N,L}_{n}}{q_{n}}\leq\frac{\sum_{n\leq cN^{2/d}}\rho^{N,L}_{n}}{\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\{-\pi c(\rho^{1/d}\lambda_{\beta})^{2}z^{2}\}}+\sum_{n>cN^{2/d}}\rho^{N,L}_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\rho}{\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\{-\pi c(\rho^{1/d}\lambda_{\beta})^{2}z^{2}\}}+\sum_{n>cN^{2/d}}\rho^{N,L}_{n}

(2.18)

because
q_{n}>1
. Taking the limit,

\rho_{0}\leq\frac{\rho}{\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\{-\pi c(\rho^{1/d}\lambda_{\beta})^{2}z^{2}\}}

(2.19)

for
c
arbitrarily small, therefore
\rho_{0}=0
.
\quad\Box

The second step can be performed in many different ways. We present one which will have its analogue in the case of interacting bosons.

Theorem 2.2
.

If
\rho\leq\zeta(d/2)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}
then in the infinite system there are only finite cycles. If
\rho>\zeta(d/2)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}
then

\rho_{0}=\rho-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{n>\varepsilon N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}>0.

(2.20)

Proof.

Note first that with
N
and
L
increasing
Q^{0}_{N,L}=\exp\{-\beta F^{0}_{N,L}\}=\exp\{-\beta L^{d}[f^{0}(\rho,\beta)+o(1)]\}
. Furthermore, if
n=o(N)
then

\frac{Q^{0}_{N-n,L}}{Q^{0}_{N,L}}=e^{\beta n\frac{F^{0}_{N,L}/L^{d}-F^{0}_{N-n,L}/L^{d}}{(n/L^{d})}}=e^{\beta n[\partial f^{0}(\rho,\beta)/\partial\rho+o(1)]}.

(2.21)

In
\partial f^{0}(\rho,\beta)/\partial\rho
we recognise the chemical potential computed in the canonical ensemble.
Let
g_{N}=o(N^{2/d})
be any monotone increasing sequence of integers that tends to infinity.
Applying the second form of
q_{n}
,

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{g_{N}}q_{n}\frac{Q^{0}_{N-n,L}}{Q^{0}_{N,L}}=\frac{1}{\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\sum_{n=1}^{g_{N}}\frac{1}{n^{d/2}}\left[1+O\left(e^{-\pi L^{2}/(n\lambda_{\beta}^{2})}\right)\right]e^{\beta n[\partial f^{0}(\rho,\beta)/\partial\rho+o(1)]}.

(2.22)

We cut the sum in two parts,
\sum_{n=1}^{M}+\sum_{n=M+1}^{g_{N}}
and take
\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}
resulting

\displaystyle\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{g_{N}}q_{n}\frac{Q^{0}_{N-n,L}}{Q^{0}_{N,L}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\exp\{\beta n\partial f^{0}(\rho,\beta)/\partial\rho\}}{n^{d/2}}

(2.23)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=M+1}^{g_{N}}q_{n}\frac{Q^{0}_{N-n,L}}{Q^{0}_{N,L}}.

The infinite sum contains the contribution of all the finite cycles. The rest comes from cycles whose length although diverges with
N
, but the divergence is slower than
N^{2/d}
, and the double limit yields zero:

\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}\rho}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=M+1}^{g_{N}}q_{n}\frac{Q^{0}_{N-n,L}}{Q^{0}_{N,L}}=\frac{1}{\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}\rho}\sum_{n=M+1}^{g_{N}}\frac{1}{n^{d/2}}\frac{Q^{0}_{N-n,L}}{Q^{0}_{N,L}}=0.

Next, fix any
c>0
and choose any monotone increasing sequence
h_{N}
of integers such that
h_{N}>cN^{2/d}
and
h_{N}=o(N)
. Then

\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=cN^{2/d}}^{h_{N}}q_{n}\frac{Q^{0}_{N-n,L}}{Q^{0}_{N,L}}=0

(2.24)

because
q_{n}Q^{0}_{N-n,L}/Q^{0}_{N,L}=O(1)
. Combining Eqs. (
2.23
) and (
2.24
),

1=\frac{1}{\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\exp\{\beta n\partial f^{0}(\rho,\beta)/\partial\rho\}}{n^{d/2}}+\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=\varepsilon N}^{N}\frac{Q^{0}_{N-n,L}}{Q^{0}_{N,L}}.

(2.25)

We used also that
q_{n}\to 1
if
n
increases faster than
N^{2/d}
.
Now
f^{0}
is a negative strictly monotone decreasing function of
\rho
below the critical density

\zeta(d/2)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}
. Therefore, for
\rho\leq\zeta(d/2)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}
,
\varepsilon>0
and
n\geq\varepsilon N

\frac{Q^{0}_{N-n,L}}{Q^{0}_{N,L}}\sim e^{-\beta L^{d}\left[f^{0}((1-n/N)\rho,\beta)-f^{0}(\rho,\beta)\right]}\leq e^{-\beta L^{d}\left[f^{0}((1-\varepsilon)\rho,\beta)-f^{0}(\rho,\beta)\right]}

(2.26)

decays exponentially fast with the volume,
N^{-1}\sum_{n=\varepsilon N}^{N}Q^{0}_{N-n,L}/Q^{0}_{N,L}
also decays at this rate and turns Eq. (
2.25
) into

\frac{1}{\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\exp\{\beta n\partial f^{0}(\rho,\beta)/\partial\rho\}}{n^{d/2}}=1\qquad(\rho\leq\zeta(d/2)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}).

(2.27)

This equation determines
\partial f^{0}(\rho,\beta)/\partial\rho
and assigns to it a non-positive value.
At
\rho=\zeta(d/2)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}
the sum over
n
and
\partial f^{0}(\rho,\beta)/\partial\rho
reach their maxima,
\zeta(d/2)
and zero, respectively.
Therefore, if
\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}>\zeta(d/2)
, Eq. (
2.25
) can hold only with a positive contribution from macroscopic cycles. For this conclusion we are not obliged to further analyse the second term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
2.25
). Still, it must be detached from zero exactly when the first term sinks below 1, and this is easy to see.
If
\rho>\zeta(d/2)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}
then
\partial f^{0}(\rho,\beta)/\partial\rho\equiv 0
. At the same time, if

(1-n/N)\rho\geq\zeta(d/2)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}
, then

f^{0}((1-n/N)\rho,\beta)=f^{0}(\rho,\beta)=-\frac{\zeta(1+d/2)}{\beta\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}
,
and
Q^{0}_{N-n,L}/Q^{0}_{N,L}\to 1
.
(In the case of periodic boundary conditions there is no surface/edge/corner correction to the limit of
Q^{0}_{N-n,L}/Q^{0}_{N,L}
.) So (
2.25
) becomes

\frac{\zeta(d/2)}{\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}+\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=\varepsilon N}^{(1-\zeta(d/2)/\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d})N}\frac{Q^{0}_{N-n,L}}{Q^{0}_{N,L}}=\frac{\zeta(d/2)}{\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}+\left(1-\frac{\zeta(d/2)}{\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\right)=1\quad(\rho>\zeta(d/2)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}).\quad\Box

Finally, we recall some more results obtained in [Su2]. First, the expected number of infinite cycles that contain at least a fraction
x
of the total number of particles is

\ln\frac{\rho_{0}}{x\rho}
for any
x<\rho_{0}/\rho
, cf. Eq. (44) of [Su2]. This number can be arbitrarily large if
x
is sufficiently small. For
m\geq\ln\rho/\rho_{0}
the expected number of infinite cycles of density between

e^{-(m+1)}\rho
and
e^{-m}\rho
is

\ln\frac{e^{m+1}\rho_{0}}{\rho}-\ln\frac{e^{m}\rho_{0}}{\rho}=1.

The intervals
[e^{-(m+1)},e^{-m})
are disjoint, their number is infinite and on average there belongs one infinite cycle to each interval.

Second, the limit shape of partitions of
N
in Vershik’s sense [V] can be inferred from Eqs. (26), (27) and (44) of [Su2]. Let
r_{k}(\lambda)
denote the number of elements of length
k
in the partition
\lambda
of
N
. Then the limit measure over the set of partitions is singular,

\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\frac{r_{k}(\lambda)}{N}=\frac{1}{k}\frac{\rho_{k}}{\rho}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\frac{z^{k}}{k^{d/2+1}\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}&\mbox{if}&\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}\leq\zeta(d/2)\\
\frac{1}{k^{d/2+1}\rho^{0}_{c}(\beta)\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}=\frac{1}{k^{d/2+1}\zeta(d/2)}&\mbox{if}&\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}>\zeta(d/2)\end{array}\right.

(2.28)

with probability one, where
z
is the solution of

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n^{d/2}}=\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}.

Thus, with the scaling factor
a=1
the limit shape for the finite elements of the partitions is

\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t):=\frac{a}{N}\sum_{k\geq at}r_{k}(\lambda)\to\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\frac{1}{\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\sum_{k\geq t}\frac{z^{k}}{k^{d/2+1}}&\mbox{if}&\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}\leq\zeta(d/2)\\
\frac{1}{\zeta(d/2)}\sum_{k\geq t}\frac{1}{k^{d/2+1}}&\mbox{if}&\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}>\zeta(d/2).\end{array}\right.

(2.29)

Furthermore, when
\rho\lambda_{\beta}^{d}>\zeta(d/2)
, with the scaling sequence
a_{N}=N\rho^{N,L}_{0}/\rho
we obtain the limit shape for the macroscopic elements of the partitions : dropping the prefactor
\rho^{N,L}_{0}/\rho
,

\tilde{\varphi}^{\rm macr}_{\lambda}(t)\propto\sum_{k\geq(\rho^{N,L}_{0}/\rho)Nt}r_{k}(\lambda)\to\left(\ln\frac{1}{t}\right)_{+}

(2.30)

with probability one.

3
Remarks to Lemma
1.1

Remark 1
.

In the proof we will obtain

\int_{0}^{1}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,\alpha^{k}_{j}}\int_{0}^{t^{k}_{j,\alpha^{k}_{j}}}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,\alpha^{k}_{j}-1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t^{k}_{j,2}}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,1}

and replace it with the symmetric

\frac{1}{\alpha^{k}_{j}!}\int_{0}^{1}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,1}\,{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,2}\cdots{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,\alpha^{k}_{j}}\ .

This can be done because in the formula (
1.1
) the times
t^{k}_{j,r}
are compared to
t
, not to each other, therefore any permutation of
t^{k}_{j,1},\dots,t^{k}_{j,\alpha^{k}_{j}}
rearranges only the sums with respect to
r
.

Remark 2
.

The exponents in Eq. (
1.37
) can be expanded yielding

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\int_{0}^{1}\left[z+Z_{q}(t)\right]^{2}{\,\rm d}t\right\}

(3.1)

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left[\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right\}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z+\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)^{2}\right\}

where

\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}=\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\int_{0}^{1}Z_{q}(t){\,\rm d}t,\quad\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\int_{0}^{1}Z_{q}(t)^{2}{\,\rm d}t.

(3.2)

Performing the time integral and interchanging the order of summations,

\displaystyle n_{l}\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}=\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\int_{0}^{1}Z_{q}(t){\,\rm d}t=-\sum_{j=1}^{N_{l-1}}\sum_{k\in C_{l}}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}(k-N_{l-1}-1+t^{k}_{j,r})z^{k}_{j,r}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\{j<k\}\subset C_{l}}(k-j)\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}z^{k}_{j,r}+\sum_{j\in C_{l}}\sum_{k=N_{l}+1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}(j-N_{l-1}-1+t^{k}_{j,r})z^{k}_{j,r}

\displaystyle\equiv-\sum_{k\in C_{l}}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}\left(k-N_{l-1}-1+t^{k}_{j,r}\right)z^{k}_{j,r}+\sum_{j\in C_{l}}\sum_{k=j+1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}\left(j-N_{l-1}-1+t^{k}_{j,r}\right)z^{k}_{j,r}.

The second form is derived differently, see the proof of the lemma, but the identity is evident if we write

k-j=(k-N_{l-1}-1+t^{k}_{j,r})-(j-N_{l-1}-1+t^{k}_{j,r}).

The formula for
\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}
is lengthier and not very illuminating. It can be obtained by integration with respect to
t
, but there is still another way described in the proof of the lemma. The special cases needed in the proof of the theorems will be given there.

Remark 3
.

\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}=0
if and only if
\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}=\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}=0
and this holds if and only if

\alpha^{k}_{j}=0\quad\mbox{whenever}\quad\{j,k\}\cap C_{l}\neq\emptyset.

(3.4)

Equation (
3.4
) implies
Z_{q}(t)\equiv 0
and hence
\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}=\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}=0
.
For a proof in the other direction one must inspect Eqs. (
1.1
), (
1.40
) and (
3.2
).
\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}=0
if and only if
Z_{q}(t)
is the same constant for all
q\in C_{l}
for almost all
t\in[0,1]
. The times
t^{k}_{j,r}
occurring in (
1.1
) are almost surely positive, so there is an interval above 0 in which
Z_{N_{l-1}+1}(t)=Z_{N_{l-1}+1}(0)=Z^{l}_{1}
; therefore
Z_{q}(t)=Z^{l}_{1}
(
=0
because of
\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}
)
for all
q\in C_{l}
for almost all
t\in[0,1]
. This however needs the condition (
3.4
).

A physical interpretation is the following.
\hbar(2\pi/L)Z_{q}(t)
may be considered as the shift due to interactions of the momentum of the
q
th particle at ”time”
t
compared to its value in the ideal gas. The shift does not fluctuate if the interaction does not fluctuate, like when the particle is exposed only to a constant external field. This is precisely what (
3.4
) means, cf. the next remark.

Remark 4
.

Z^{l}_{1}=0
will be seen as a consequence of the closing of the
l
th trajectory. If
x=0
, this holds for all the trajectories; if
x\neq 0
, the trajectories
l=1,\dots,p
are closed, but the zeroth trajectory is open. Nonetheless,
Z^{0}_{1}=0
, because the general formula (
1.40
) is valid for
l=0
and because
\sum_{l=0}^{p}Z^{l}_{1}\equiv 0
. (
Z^{0}_{1}
must be zero, otherwise
G\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x)
would not be continuous at
x=0
.)
The constraint is absent in the mean-field approximation which consists in keeping a single term,

\alpha^{k}_{j}=0
for all
1\leq j<k\leq N
. For
x=0
this yields the partition function of the mean-field Bose gas,

Q_{N,L}^{\rm m.f.}=\exp\left\{-\frac{\beta\rho\hat{u}(0)(N-1)}{2}\right\}\sum_{p=1}^{N}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\geq 1:\sum_{1}^{p}n_{l}=N}\ \prod_{l=1}^{p}\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}z^{2}\right\},

(3.5)

differing from that of the noninteracting gas only in the first factor.
One can go beyond the mean-field approximation and still avoid the constraint.
Z^{l}_{1}
depends only on
z^{k}_{j,r}
where one and only one of
j
and
k
is in

C_{l},

i.e., in the
l
th cycle. A meaningful completion of the mean-field result is to add terms in which the cycles are decoupled by retaining only
\alpha^{k}_{j}=0
if
j
and
k
are in different cycles. The
cycle-decoupling model
thus obtained plays a central role in the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

To go even further,

A_{l}:=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{l-1}}\sum_{k\in C_{l}}\alpha^{k}_{j}+\sum_{j\in C_{l}}\sum_{k=N_{l}+1}^{N}\alpha^{k}_{j}\geq 2

(3.6)

is necessary to satisfy
Z^{l}_{1}=0
with nonzero vectors. Such an equation cannot stand alone: if
\alpha^{k}_{j}>0
, one of
j
and
k
is in another cycle
l^{\prime}
, implying a coupled equation
Z^{l^{\prime}}_{1}=0
. Suppose that
l_{1}<\cdots<l_{s}
(
s\geq 2
) label a maximal set of coupled permutation cycles, giving rise to
s
homogeneous linear equations. Clearly,
s\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{s}A_{l_{i}}
, the number of involved pairs
(j,k)
at
\alpha^{k}_{j}
different times, i.e., the total number of variables in the
s
equations. Explicitly, these variables are

\bigcup_{1\leq i^{\prime}<i\leq s}\left\{z^{k}_{j,r}|j\in C_{l_{i^{\prime}}},\ k\in C_{l_{i}},\ r=1,\dots,\alpha^{k}_{j}\right\}.

In general, if
j
is in cycle
l^{\prime}
and
k
is in cycle
l>l^{\prime}
,
z^{k}_{j,r}
appears in two equations with different signs: as
+z^{k}_{j,r}
in
Z^{l^{\prime}}_{1}=0
and as
-z^{k}_{j,r}
in
Z^{l}_{1}=0
. For a given set
\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}
the system of equations may not be soluble with all
z^{k}_{j,r}
nonzero. Such an
\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}
is illicit and is discarded by
\prod_{l=0}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}
. On the other hand, if
\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}
is a valid set and if the number of linearly independent equations is
K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}
then
\prod_{l=0}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}
makes
K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}
sums over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
collapse into a single vector leaving a factor
(L^{-d})^{K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}}
uncompensated by sums. This appears as a loss of weight in
Q_{N,L}
compared to the mean-field and other contributions with uncoupled cycles.

A systematic study of the constraint can be done with the help of graph theory. Consider a graph
{\cal G}_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}
of
p+1
vertices numbered from 0 to
p
with

b_{l^{\prime}l}=\sum_{j\in C_{l^{\prime}}}\sum_{k\in C_{l}}\alpha^{k}_{j}

edges between the vertices
l^{\prime}
and
l>l^{\prime}
. The edge set is valid if to every edge one can assign a nonzero vector so that at any vertex
l
the sum of the vectors on the incident edges
(l,l_{1}),(l,l_{2}),\dots
, taken with minus sign if
l_{i}<l
and with plus sign if
l_{i}>l
, is zero.
A solution to this graph problem together with examples is presented in the proof of Lemma
1.1
.
Here is the result:

A set
\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}
is compatible with the constraint
\prod_{l=0}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}
if and only if every maximal connected subgraph of
{\cal G}_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}
is either a single vertex or a merger through vertices or along edges of circular graphs of arbitrary length.

A simple argument shows (see Proposition
4.3
) that for a connected merger graph of
V
vertices the number
K
of independent equations for the edge variables is
V-1
, whatever be the number of edges. Let the
\sum_{0\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}b_{l^{\prime}l}

edges of
{\cal G}_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}
couple the
p+1
vertices into
m_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}
maximal connected subgraphs of
V_{1},\dots,V_{m_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}}
vertices, respectively. The
i
th one contributes to
K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}
with
V_{i}-1
, so

K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}}(V_{i}-1)=p+1-m_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}.

(3.7)

The cycles can be coupled in so many different ways that the coupling always overcompensates the loss and results in a decrease of the free energy density. This will be seen in the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Remark 5
.

Asymptotically, for
L
so large that the Riemann integral-approximating sum
L^{-d}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\hat{u}(z/L)
can be replaced with
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\hat{u}(x){\,\rm d}x
, by the substitutions
x^{k}_{j,r}=z^{k}_{j,r}/L
,
X^{l}_{1}=Z^{l}_{1}/L
and
X_{q}(t)=Z_{q}(t)/L
one obtains

\displaystyle G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{0}^{p}\right](x)=\exp\left\{-\frac{\beta\hat{u}(0)N(N-1)}{2L^{d}}\right\}\sum_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}|1\leq j<k\leq N\}}\ \Delta_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\},\{n_{l}\}}\left(L^{-d}\right)^{K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}}\prod_{1\leq j<k\leq N}\frac{\left(-\beta\right)^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}}{\alpha^{k}_{j}!}

\displaystyle\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}\int_{0}^{1}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,r}\int{\,\rm d}x^{k}_{j,r}\ \hat{u}\left(x^{k}_{j,r}\right)\left[\delta(X^{0}_{1},\dots,X^{p}_{1})\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\sum_{q=1}^{n_{0}-1}\int_{0}^{1}\left[z+LX_{q}(t)\right]^{2}{\,\rm d}t\right\}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{L}z\cdot x\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\prod_{l=1}^{p}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\int_{0}^{1}\left[z+LX_{q}(t)\right]^{2}{\,\rm d}t\right\}\right].

(3.8)

The factor
(L^{-d})^{K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}}
appears explicitly because

\displaystyle\left[\prod_{1\leq j<k\leq N}\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{z^{k}_{j,r}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\hat{u}\left(\frac{z^{k}_{j,r}}{L}\right)\right]\prod_{l=0}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}

(3.9)

\displaystyle\sim\left(L^{-d}\right)^{K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}}\int\hat{u}\left(x^{k}_{j,r}\right)\delta(X^{0}_{1},\dots,X^{p}_{1})\prod_{1\leq j<k\leq N}\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\,\rm d}x^{k}_{j,r}

where
\delta(X^{0}_{1},\dots,X^{p}_{1})

restricts the multiple integral to a
d\left(\sum_{j<k}\alpha^{k}_{j}-K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}\right)
-dimensional manifold on which
X^{0}_{1}=\cdots=X^{p}_{1}=0
.
Moreover,
\Delta_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\},\{n_{l}\}}=1
if
{\cal G}_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}
is a merger graph and is zero otherwise.
As in Eqs. (
3.1
) and (
3.2
),

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\int_{0}^{1}\left[z+LX_{q}(t)\right]^{2}{\,\rm d}t\right\}

(3.10)

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left[\overline{\left(X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right\}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z+L\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)^{2}\right\}

and

\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}=\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\int_{0}^{1}X_{q}(t){\,\rm d}t,\quad\overline{\left(X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\int_{0}^{1}X_{q}(t)^{2}{\,\rm d}t

(3.11)

with the replacements
\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}=\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}/L
,
\overline{(X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}})^{2}}=\overline{(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}})^{2}}/L^{2}
.

4
Proof of Lemma
1.1

The main steps of the proof are as follows.

(1) Discrete-time approximation: the time integrals of the pair potentials are replaced by their Riemann approximating sum. Then the Wiener measure depends only on a finite sequence of values that the trajectory assumes in the selected instants, and appears as a product of a finite number of (periodized) Gaussians.

(2) Each Gaussian and each Boltzmann factor is Fourier-expanded, making possible the integration with respect to the spatial variables. This introduces relations among the Fourier variables and permits to eliminate those associated with the Gaussians, i.e., with the kinetic energy.

(3) One takes the limit that restores the continuous time.

First we prove the formula for
G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]
, cf. Eq. (
1.33
).

4.1
Discrete-time approximation

The interval
[0,\beta]
is divided into
m
subintervals of length
\beta/m
. Let

x^{l}_{km+i}=\omega_{l}((km+i)\beta/m),\qquad l=1,\ldots,p,\quad k=0,\ldots,n_{l}-1,\quad i=1,\ldots,m,\qquad x^{l}_{n_{l}m}\equiv x^{l}_{0}=x_{l}.

(4.1)

Thus, the spatial integration variables will be
x^{l}_{1},\dots,x^{l}_{n_{l}m}
for
l=1\dots,p
. Then, with the notation

E_{m}(x)=\exp\left\{-\frac{\beta}{m}u_{L}(x)\right\}

(4.2)

the discrete-time approximation of
G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]
is

\displaystyle G_{m}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]=\int_{\Lambda^{mN}}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\prod_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}{\,\rm d}x^{l}_{j}\int\prod_{l=1}^{p}\prod_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}W^{\beta/m}_{x^{l}_{j-1}x^{l}_{j}}({\,\rm d}\omega^{l}_{j})\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\ \prod_{0\leq j<k\leq n_{l}-1}E_{m}(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{l}_{jm+i})

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}\,\prod_{1\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}\prod_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}\prod_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}E_{m}(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{l^{\prime}}_{jm+i}).

(4.3)

4.2
Fourier expansions

Let us start with the Wiener measure.

\int W^{\beta/m}_{xy}({\,\rm d}\omega)=\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\int P^{\beta/m}_{x,y+Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)=\lambda_{\beta/m}^{-d}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}e^{-\pi(x-y+Lz)^{2}/\lambda_{\beta/m}^{2}}=\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}e^{-\pi\lambda_{\beta/m}^{2}z^{2}/L^{2}}e^{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}z\cdot(x-y)}.

(4.4)

The rightmost form, obtained via the Poisson summation formula, is the Fourier representation we need. Denoting the dual vector of
x^{l}_{j}-x^{l}_{j-1}
by
v^{l}_{j,j-1}
(
j=1,\dots,n_{l}m
),

\displaystyle\int\prod_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}W^{\beta/m}_{x^{l}_{j-1}x^{l}_{j}}({\,\rm d}\omega^{l}_{j})=\frac{1}{L^{dn_{l}m}}\sum_{v^{l}_{10},v^{l}_{21},\ldots,v^{l}_{n_{l}m,n_{l}m-1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{mL^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}(v^{l}_{j,j-1})^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\times\exp\left\{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}v^{l}_{j,j-1}\cdot(x^{l}_{j}-x^{l}_{j-1})\right\}.

(4.5)

Now

\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}v^{l}_{j,j-1}\cdot(x^{l}_{j}-x^{l}_{j-1})=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}(v^{l}_{j,j-1}-v^{l}_{j+1,j})\cdot x^{l}_{j}\quad\mbox{where}\quad v^{l}_{n_{l}m+1,n_{l}m}\equiv v^{l}_{10}.

(4.6)

Introduce

v^{l}_{j}=v^{l}_{j,j-1}-v^{l}_{j+1,j}\ ,\quad j=1,\ldots,n_{l}m.

(4.7)

There are only
n_{l}m-1
linearly independent differences,

\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}v^{l}_{j}=0.

(4.8)

The new variables
\{v^{l}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n_{l}m-1}
together with
v^{l}_{10}
form a linearly independent set. Utilizing them,

v^{l}_{j,j-1}=v^{l}_{10}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{n_{l}m}v^{l}_{j^{\prime}},\qquad j=1,\ldots,n_{l}m,

(4.9)

\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}(v^{l}_{j,j-1})^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}m}(v^{l}_{10}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{n_{l}m}v^{l}_{j^{\prime}})^{2},

(4.10)

\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}v^{l}_{j,j-1}\cdot(x^{l}_{j}-x^{l}_{j-1})=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}v^{l}_{j}\cdot x^{l}_{j}.

(4.11)

Substituting the last two expressions into Eq. (
4.2
) and replacing the summation variables
v^{l}_{j+1,j}
with
v^{l}_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,n_{l}m-1
while keeping
v^{l}_{10}
,

\displaystyle\int\prod_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}W^{\beta/m}_{x^{l}_{j-1}x^{l}_{j}}({\,\rm d}\omega^{l}_{j})=\frac{1}{L^{dn_{l}m}}\sum_{v^{l}_{10}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\ \sum_{v^{l}_{1},v^{l}_{2},\ldots,v^{l}_{n_{l}m-1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{mL^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}(v^{l}_{10}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{n_{l}m}v^{l}_{j^{\prime}})^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}\exp\left\{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}x^{l}_{j}\cdot v^{l}_{j}\right\}.

(4.12)

The Fourier expansion of the Boltzmann factors is

E_{m}(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{l^{\prime}}_{jm+i})=\sum_{z_{l^{\prime}j}^{lk}(i)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\hat{E}_{m}\left(z_{l^{\prime}j}^{lk}(i)\right)\exp\left\{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}z_{l^{\prime}j}^{lk}(i)\cdot(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{l^{\prime}}_{jm+i})\right\}.

(4.13)

Here

\hat{E}_{m}(z)=\frac{1}{L^{d}}\int_{\Lambda}\exp\left\{-{\mathrm{i}}\frac{2\pi}{L}z\cdot x\right\}\exp\left\{-\frac{\beta}{m}u_{L}(x)\right\}{\,\rm d}x=\delta_{z,0}-\frac{\beta}{mL^{d}}\ \hat{u}(z/L)+\frac{1}{L^{d}}O(1/m^{2})

(4.14)

with

\hat{u}(z/L)=\int\exp\left\{-{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}z\cdot x\right\}u(x){\,\rm d}x.

(4.15)

In Eq. (
4.14
) after the Taylor-expansion of
\exp\{-(\beta/m)u_{L}(x)\}
we substituted (
1.3
). The summation over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
combined with the integral over
\Lambda
is equivalent to integrating over the whole space.
Notice that
\hat{E}_{m}(z)=\hat{E}_{m}(-z)=\hat{E}_{m}(z)^{*}
. This holds true
because
u_{L}(x)=u_{L}(-x)
. Similarly,
\hat{u}(z/L)
and also the remainder are real even functions of
z
. Some short notations will be useful:

{\cal Z}_{l}=\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\right)^{mn_{l}(n_{l}-1)/2},\quad{\cal Z}_{<}=\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\right)^{m\sum_{1\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}n_{l^{\prime}}n_{l}},\quad{\cal Z}=\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\right)^{mN(N-1)/2}.

(4.16)

The elements of these sets are considered as sets of vectors from
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
,
and the notation

\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}=\prod_{z\in Z}\hat{E}_{m}(z)

(4.17)

will be used for
Z\in{\cal Z}_{l}
,
{\cal Z}_{<}
,
{\cal Z}
.
Now

\prod_{i=1}^{m}\ \prod_{0\leq j<k\leq n_{l}-1}E_{m}(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{l}_{jm+i})=\sum_{Z\in{\cal Z}_{l}}\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\exp\left\{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{l}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}z^{lk}_{lj}(i)\cdot(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{l}_{jm+i})\right\}

(4.18)

which, after substituting

\sum_{k=1}^{n_{l}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}z^{lk}_{lj}(i)\cdot(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{l}_{jm+i})=\sum_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}x^{l}_{km+i}\cdot\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}z^{lk}_{lj}(i)-\sum_{j=k+1}^{n_{l}-1}z^{lj}_{lk}(i)\right)

(4.19)

results in

\prod_{i=1}^{m}\ \prod_{0\leq j<k\leq n_{l}-1}E_{m}(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{l}_{jm+i})=\sum_{Z\in{\cal Z}_{l}}\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}\exp\left\{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}x^{l}_{km+i}\cdot\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}z^{lk}_{lj}(i)-\sum_{j=k+1}^{n_{l}-1}z^{lj}_{lk}(i)\right)\right\}.

(4.20)

Furthermore,

\sum_{1\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}\sum_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}z^{lk}_{l^{\prime}j}(i)\cdot\left(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{l^{\prime}}_{jm+i}\right)=\sum_{l=1}^{p}\sum_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}x^{l}_{km+i}\cdot\left(\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{l-1}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}z^{lk}_{l^{\prime}j}(i)-\sum_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{p}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}z^{l^{\prime}j}_{lk}(i)\right),

(4.21)

so

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}\,\prod_{1\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}\prod_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}\prod_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}E_{m}(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{l^{\prime}}_{jm+i})

(4.22)

\displaystyle=\sum_{Z\in{\cal Z}_{<}}\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\prod_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}\exp\left\{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}x^{l}_{km+i}\cdot\left(\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{l-1}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}z^{lk}_{l^{\prime}j}(i)-\sum_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{p}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}z^{l^{\prime}j}_{lk}(i)\right)\right\}.

From Eqs. (
4.2
), (
4.20
) and (
4.22
) one can collect the multiplier of
x^{l}_{km+i}
.
If we write
G_{m}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]
as

G_{m}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]=\int_{\Lambda^{mN}}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\prod_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}{\,\rm d}x^{l}_{j}\ G\left[\left\{(x^{l}_{j})_{j=1}^{n_{l}m},n_{l}\right\}_{l=1}^{p}\right]

(4.23)

then

\displaystyle G\left[\left\{(x^{l}_{j})_{j=1}^{n_{l}m},n_{l}\right\}_{l=1}^{p}\right]=\sum_{Z\in{\cal Z}}\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{p}\left\{\sum_{v^{l}_{10}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\ \sum_{v^{l}_{1},v^{l}_{2},\ldots,v^{l}_{n_{l}m-1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\left[e^{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{mL^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}(v^{l}_{10}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{n_{l}m}v^{l}_{j^{\prime}})^{2}}\prod_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{L^{d}}e^{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}x^{l}_{km+i}\cdot(v^{l}_{km+i}-z^{l}_{km+i})}\right]\right\}

where

z^{l}_{km+i}=-\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}z^{lk}_{lj}(i)+\sum_{j=k+1}^{n_{l}-1}z^{lj}_{lk}(i)-\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{l-1}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}z^{lk}_{l^{\prime}j}(i)+\sum_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{p}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}z^{l^{\prime}j}_{lk}(i).

(4.25)

The multiple sum (
4.2
) is absolutely convergent and can be integrated term by term. In each term there is factorization according to the spatial variables
x^{l}_{km+i}
, so the integration of the complex units can be made separately.
Integration with respect to
x^{l}_{j}
in
\Lambda
makes
v^{l}_{j}
coincide with
z^{l}_{j}
for all
j

and turns Eq. (
4.8
) into

\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}z^{l}_{j}=0.

(4.26)

Thus,

G_{m}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]=\sum_{Z\in{\cal Z}}\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\delta_{\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}z^{l}_{j},0}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{mL^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}(z+\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{n_{l}m}z^{l}_{j^{\prime}})^{2}\right\}.

(4.27)

Finally, defining

Z^{l}_{j}=\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{n_{l}m}z^{l}_{j^{\prime}},\qquad\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}=\frac{1}{n_{l}m}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}Z^{l}_{j},\qquad\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{n_{l}m}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}(Z^{l}_{j})^{2},

(4.28)

where we use the same notations for the averages as for their
m\to\infty
limit in (
3.1
), Eq. (
4.27
) becomes

\displaystyle G_{m}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]=\sum_{Z\in{\cal Z}}\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{mL^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}(z+Z^{l}_{j})^{2}\right\}

(4.29)

\displaystyle=\sum_{Z\in{\cal Z}}\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left[\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right\}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z+\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)^{2}\right\}.

A consequence of
Z^{l}_{1}=0
is that
\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}=0
if and only if
Z^{l}_{j}=0
for
j=1,\ldots,n_{l}m
which holds if and only if
z^{l}_{j}=0
for
j=1,\ldots,n_{l}m
. (Note:
Z^{l}_{j}=-\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{j-1}z^{l}_{j^{\prime}}
as well, cf. Eq. (
4.26
).) This is the finite-
m
equivalent of Remark
3
in Section 3.
Two identities in the individual
z
vectors are noteworthy because they hold for every
i
:

\sum_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}z^{l}_{km+i}=-\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{l-1}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}\sum_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}z^{lk}_{l^{\prime}j}(i)+\sum_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{p}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{l^{\prime}}-1}\sum_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}z^{l^{\prime}j}_{lk}(i),

(4.30)

i.e. the intra-cycle contribution vanishes from the sum on the left-hand side, and

\sum_{l=1}^{p}\sum_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}z^{l}_{km+i}=0.

(4.31)

Summing this latter with respect to
i
yields
\sum_{l=1}^{p}Z^{l}_{1}\equiv 0
.

At this point it is useful to change the notation and number the particles continuously from 1 to
N
. Particle
lk
, the
k
th particle (starting from 0) of the
l
th cycle will carry the number
N_{l-1}+k+1
when counted continuously, so the identities new
\equiv
old are

z^{N_{l-1}+k+1}_{N_{l^{\prime}-1}+j+1}(i)\equiv z^{lk}_{l^{\prime}j}(i)\ \mbox{or}\ z^{q}_{j^{\prime}}(i)\equiv z^{l,q-N_{l-1}-1}_{l^{\prime},j^{\prime}-N_{l^{\prime}-1}-1}(i),\quad z_{N_{l-1}+k+1}(i)\equiv z^{l}_{km+i}\ \mbox{or}\ z_{q}(i)\equiv z^{l}_{(q-N_{l-1}-1)m+i}

(4.32)

for
q\in C_{l}
and
j^{\prime}\in C_{l^{\prime}}
.
The new notation is better suited to
\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}
which is independent of the cycle structure. Also, the expression (
4.25
) is replaced with the cycle-independent

z_{q}(i)=-\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}z^{q}_{j}(i)+\sum_{k=q+1}^{N}z^{k}_{q}(i),\qquad q=1,\ldots,N,\quad i=1,\ldots,m,

(4.33)

and the expanded form of
Z^{l}_{j}
,
\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
and
\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}
in terms of the individual
z
vectors becomes more transparent. For
q\in C_{l}
one finds

\displaystyle Z^{l}_{(q-N_{l-1}-1)m+i}=\sum_{i^{\prime}=i}^{m}\sum_{k=q}^{N_{l}}z_{k}(i^{\prime})+\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}\sum_{k=q+1}^{N_{l}}z_{k}(i^{\prime})

(4.34)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime}=i}^{m}\left[-\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}\sum_{k=q}^{N_{l}}z^{k}_{j}(i^{\prime})+\sum_{j=q}^{N_{l}}\sum_{k=N_{l}+1}^{N}z^{k}_{j}(i^{\prime})\right]+\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}\left[-\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=q+1}^{N_{l}}z^{k}_{j}(i^{\prime})+\sum_{j=q+1}^{N_{l}}\sum_{k=N_{l}+1}^{N}z^{k}_{j}(i^{\prime})\right].

In particular,

Z^{l}_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left[-\sum_{j=1}^{N_{l-1}}\sum_{k\in C_{l}}z^{k}_{j}(i)+\sum_{j\in C_{l}}\sum_{k=N_{l}+1}^{N}z^{k}_{j}(i)\right].

(4.35)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}=\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(q-N_{l-1}-1+\frac{i}{m}\right)z_{q}(i)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\left[-\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(q-N_{l-1}-1+\frac{i}{m}\right)\,z^{q}_{j}(i)+\sum_{k=q+1}^{N}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(q-N_{l-1}-1+\frac{i}{m}\right)\,z^{k}_{q}(i)\right]

and

\displaystyle\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=0}^{n_{l}-1}\ \sum_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{m}\min\left\{k+\frac{i}{m},k^{\prime}+\frac{i^{\prime}}{m}\right\}z^{l}_{km+i}\cdot z^{l}_{k^{\prime}m+i^{\prime}}

(4.37)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}\in C_{l}}\ \sum_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{m}\left(\min\left\{q+\frac{i}{m},q^{\prime}+\frac{i^{\prime}}{m}\right\}-N_{l-1}-1\right)z_{q}(i)\cdot z_{q^{\prime}}(i^{\prime}).

The last formula becomes complete after (
4.33
) is substituted in it.

4.3
The case
x\neq 0

For the zeroth cycle we have to insert

L^{d}\int W_{0x}^{n\beta}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{0})}\prod_{l=1}^{p}e^{-\beta U(\omega_{0},\omega_{l})}.

The formulas or parts of formulas not involving cycle 0 are unchanged. There are
nm-1
new integration variables
x^{0}_{j}=\omega_{0}(j\beta/m)
,
j=1,\dots,nm-1
completed with the fixed
x^{0}_{0}=0
and
x^{0}_{nm}=x
. The new Fourier variables associated with differences of position vectors are:
v_{j,j-1}
for
x^{0}_{j}-x^{0}_{j-1}
(
j=1,\dots,nm
),
z^{0k}_{0j}(i)
for
x^{0}_{km+i}-x^{0}_{jm+i}
(
0\leq j<k\leq n-1
,
i=1,\dots,m
) and
z^{lk}_{0j}(i)
for
x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{0}_{jm+i}
(
l=1,\dots,p
,
k=0,\dots,n_{l}-1
,
j=0,\dots,n-1
,
i=1,\dots,m
). We have

\sum_{j=1}^{nm}v^{0}_{j,j-1}\cdot(x^{0}_{j}-x^{0}_{j-1})=\sum_{j=1}^{nm-1}(v^{0}_{j,j-1}-v^{0}_{j+1,j})\cdot x^{0}_{j}+v^{0}_{nm,nm-1}\cdot x=\sum_{j=1}^{nm-1}v^{0}_{j}\cdot x^{0}_{j}+v^{0}_{nm}\cdot x,

(4.38)

the second equality defining
v^{0}_{j}
. In contrast to (
4.8
), their sum is nonzero,

\sum_{j=1}^{nm}v^{0}_{j}=v^{0}_{10}.

(4.39)

Defining
v^{0}_{nm+1,nm}=0
,

v^{0}_{j,j-1}=\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{nm}v^{0}_{j^{\prime}},\quad j=1,\dots,nm,

(4.40)

so

\sum_{j=1}^{nm}\left(v^{0}_{j,j-1}\right)^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{nm}\left(\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{nm}v^{0}_{j^{\prime}}\right)^{2}

(4.41)

and

L^{d}\prod_{j=1}^{nm}\int W^{\beta/m}_{x^{0}_{j-1}x^{0}_{j}}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})=\sum_{v^{0}_{1},\dots,v^{0}_{nm}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}e^{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{mL^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{nm}\left(\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{nm}v^{0}_{j^{\prime}}\right)^{2}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{nm-1}\frac{1}{L^{d}}e^{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}v^{0}_{j}\cdot x^{0}_{j}}\right)\ e^{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}v^{0}_{nm}\cdot x}.

(4.42)

Equation (
4.25
) extends to
l=0
and thus defines
z^{0}_{j}
. Moreover, the identity (
4.30
) also holds true. It is because of this latter that the formula (
1.40
) is valid also for
l=0
with the consequence that
\sum_{l=0}^{p}Z^{l}_{1}\equiv 0
which, together with
Z^{1}_{1}=\cdots=Z^{p}_{1}=0
implies
Z^{0}_{1}=0
. Including the
z
-vectors associated with the Boltzmann factors, in the argument of the complex units
x^{0}_{j}
is scalar-multiplied with
v^{0}_{j}-z^{0}_{j}
. Integrating with respect to
x^{0}_{j}
over
\Lambda
for
j=1,\dots,nm-1
makes
v^{0}_{j}
coincide with
z^{0}_{j}
, leaving behind

\displaystyle L^{d}\int_{\Lambda^{nm-1}}\prod_{j=1}^{nm-1}{\,\rm d}x^{0}_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{nm}\int W^{\beta/m}_{x^{0}_{j-1}x^{0}_{j}}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{0\leq j<k\leq n-1}E_{m}\left(x^{0}_{km+i}-x^{0}_{jm+i}\right)\prod_{l=1}^{p}\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\prod_{k=1}^{n_{l}-1}E_{m}\left(x^{l}_{km+i}-x^{0}_{jm+i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{Z}\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}\sum_{v^{0}_{nm}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}e^{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{mL^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{nm}\left(v^{0}_{nm}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{nm-1}z^{0}_{j^{\prime}}\right)^{2}}\ e^{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}x\cdot(v^{0}_{nm}-z^{0}_{nm})}=\sum_{Z}\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}e^{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{mL^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{nm}\left(z+\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{nm}z^{0}_{j^{\prime}}\right)^{2}}\ e^{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}x\cdot z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{Z}\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}e^{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{mL^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{nm}\left(z+Z^{0}_{j}\right)^{2}}\ e^{{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}x\cdot z}.\hskip 22.76219pt

(4.43)

Above
Z=\{z^{0k}_{0j}(i)\}_{j,k,i}\cup\{z^{lk}_{0j}(i)\}_{l,j,k,i}
, each vector running over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
. Because the expression is real, we can take the real part and find the cosine appearing in the statement of the lemma.

4.4
The limit of continuous time

There is no difference between
x=0
or
x\neq 0
. We describe only the first case.
The result of the limit when
m
tends to infinity can be seen on the simplest example, that of
N=2
.
Because in this case there is a single pair, the notation can be simplified by writing
z(i)
instead of
z^{2}_{1}(i)
.
Now
{\cal Z}=({\mathbb{Z}}^{d})^{m}
whose elements are
Z=\{z(1),\ldots,z(m)\}
. There are two partitions of 2:
p=1
,
n_{1}=2
and
p=2
,
n_{1}=n_{2}=1
.

One two-particle trajectory

When
p=1
then
l=1
,
N_{l-1}=0
,
N=N_{1}=n_{1}=2
, so from (
4.33
) and (
4.35
)

z_{1}(i)=z(i),\quad z_{2}(i)=-z(i),\quad Z^{1}_{1}\equiv 0.

(4.44)

Moreover,

\overline{Z^{1}_{\cdot}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left[-\left(1+\frac{i}{m}\right)z(i)+\frac{i}{m}z(i)\right]=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}z(i),

(4.45)

\displaystyle\overline{\left(Z^{1}_{\cdot}\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{2}\sum_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{m}\left(\min\left\{j+\frac{i}{m},j^{\prime}+\frac{i^{\prime}}{m}\right\}-1\right)z_{j}(i)\cdot z_{j^{\prime}}(i^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}z(i)\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{m}\frac{|i-i^{\prime}|}{m}z(i)\cdot z(i^{\prime})

\displaystyle=2\overline{Z^{1}_{\cdot}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{m}\frac{|i-i^{\prime}|}{m}z(i)\cdot z(i^{\prime}).

(4.46)

Thus,

\overline{\left(Z^{1}_{\cdot}\right)^{2}}-\overline{Z^{1}_{\cdot}}^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{|i-j|}{2m}\right)z(i)\cdot z(j).

(4.47)

Two one-particle trajectories

In this case

Z^{1}_{1}=-Z^{2}_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}z(i)

\overline{Z^{1}_{\cdot}}=-\overline{Z^{2}_{\cdot}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{i}{m}z(i)

\overline{(Z^{1}_{\cdot})^{2}}=\overline{(Z^{2}_{\cdot})^{2}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\min\left\{\frac{i}{m},\frac{j}{m}\right\}z(i)\cdot z(j).

If
z(i)\neq 0
precisely for
\alpha
values of
i
, say, for
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\alpha}
then everywhere one can replace
\sum_{i=1}^{m}
with
\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha}
and
i
in the summand with
i_{r}
. Because
z(i_{r})
is a summation variable over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, for its labelling the value of
i_{r}
is unimportant and the notation
z_{r}
can be used for it. All this permits to rewrite

G_{m}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]=\sum_{Z\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{d})^{m}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}\right)\hat{E}_{m}^{Z}\ f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}\left(\{i/m\}_{i=1}^{m},Z\right)

(4.48)

where

f_{[2]}\left(\{i/m\}_{i=1}^{m},Z\right)=\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{|i-j|}{m}\right)z(i)\cdot z(j)\right\}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{2\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}z(i)\right)^{2}\right\}

(4.49)

and

\displaystyle f_{[1,1]}\left(\{i/m\}_{i=1}^{m},Z\right)=\exp\left\{-\frac{2\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[\min\left\{\frac{i}{m},\frac{j}{m}\right\}-\frac{ij}{m^{2}}\right]z(i)\cdot z(j)\right\}

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{i}{m}z(i)\right)^{2}\right\}\right]^{2}

(4.50)

as

\displaystyle G_{m}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z(1),\dots,z(m)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}\right)\hat{E}_{m}(z(1))\cdots\hat{E}_{m}(z(m))f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}\left(\{i/m\}_{i=1}^{m},Z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=0}^{m}\hat{E}_{m}(0)^{m-\alpha}\sum_{z_{1},\dots,z_{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}\right)\hat{E}_{m}(z_{1})\cdots\hat{E}_{m}(z_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\alpha}\leq m}\,f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}\left(\{i_{r}/m\}_{r=1}^{\alpha},\{z_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha}\right).

Now

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\alpha}\leq m}\,f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}\left(\{i_{r}/m\}_{r=1}^{\alpha},\{z_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha}\right)=\sum_{i_{\alpha}=\alpha}^{m}\ \sum_{i_{\alpha-1}=\alpha-1}^{i_{\alpha}-1}\cdots\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{i_{2}-1}f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}\left(\{i_{r}/m\}_{r=1}^{\alpha},\{z_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=m^{\alpha}\,\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i_{\alpha}=\alpha}^{m}\ \frac{1}{m}\sum_{i_{\alpha-1}=\alpha-1}^{i_{\alpha}-1}\cdots\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{i_{2}-1}f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}\left(\{i_{r}/m\}_{r=1}^{\alpha},\{z_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=m^{\alpha}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{t_{\alpha}=\alpha/m}^{1}\ \frac{1}{m}\sum_{t_{\alpha-1}=(\alpha-1)/m}^{t_{\alpha}-1/m}\cdots\frac{1}{m}\sum_{t_{1}=1/m}^{t_{2}-1/m}f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}\left(\{t_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha},\{z_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha}\right)

where each
t_{r}=i_{r}/m
varies by steps
1/m
. From Eq. (
4.14
) we substitute
\hat{E}_{m}(z)
, dropping the
O(1/m^{2})
term which disappears in the
m\to\infty
limit. This gives

\displaystyle G_{m}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{m}\left(1-\frac{\beta\hat{u}(0)}{mL^{d}}\right)^{m-\alpha}\left(\frac{-\beta}{L^{d}}\right)^{\alpha}\sum_{z_{1},\dots,z_{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}\right)\hat{u}(z_{1}/L)\cdots\hat{u}(z_{\alpha}/L)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{m}\sum_{t_{\alpha}=\alpha/m}^{1}\ \frac{1}{m}\sum_{t_{\alpha-1}=(\alpha-1)/m}^{t_{\alpha}-1/m}\cdots\frac{1}{m}\sum_{t_{1}=1/m}^{t_{2}-1/m}f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}\left(\{t_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha},\{z_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha}\right)

The summand is bounded above by

\left(\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}z^{2}\right\}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\left(\frac{\beta\sum_{z\neq 0}|\hat{u}(z/L)|}{L^{d}}\right)^{\alpha}

whose sum over
\alpha
extended to infinity is convergent. So the dominated convergence theorem applies and yields

\displaystyle G\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]=e^{-\beta\hat{u}(0)/L^{d}}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{-\beta}{L^{d}}\right)^{\alpha}\sum_{z_{1},\dots,z_{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}\right)\hat{u}(z_{1}/L)\cdots\hat{u}(z_{\alpha}/L)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{1}{\,\rm d}t_{\alpha}\int_{0}^{t_{\alpha}}{\,\rm d}t_{\alpha-1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t_{2}}{\,\rm d}t_{1}f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}\left(\{t_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha},\{z_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha}\right).

This ends the proof of the lemma for
N=2
. Note that for
p=2
the constraint
\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha}z_{r}=0
acts both on
\alpha
and on the set of variables
z_{1},\dots,z_{\alpha}
: for
\alpha=1
the sum over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
is empty and for
\alpha>1
only
\alpha-1
variables can be chosen freely from
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, meaning that these terms are of order
L^{-d}
.

For a general
N
, consider first the limit of the exponents in Eq. (
4.29
). In the first line

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{l}m}(z+Z^{l}_{j})^{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{n_{l}-1}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(z+Z^{l}_{km+i})^{2}=\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(z+Z^{l}_{(q-N_{l-1}-1)m+i})^{2}.

(4.55)

Substituting for
Z^{l}_{(q-N_{l-1}-1)m+i}
the expression (
4.34
) and keeping only the nonzero
z
vectors,

\sum_{i^{\prime}=i}^{m}z^{k}_{j}(i^{\prime})=\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\bf 1}_{\left\{i^{k}_{j,r}/m\geq i/m\right\}}z^{k}_{j}\left(i^{k}_{j,r}\right),\qquad\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}z^{k}_{j}(i^{\prime})=\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\bf 1}_{\left\{i^{k}_{j,r}/m<i/m\right\}}z^{k}_{j}\left(i^{k}_{j,r}\right).

(4.56)

When
m
tends to infinity

i/m\to t,\quad i^{k}_{j,r}/m\to t^{k}_{j,r},\quad z^{k}_{j}\left(i^{k}_{j,r}\right)\to z^{k}_{j,r},\quad Z^{l}_{(q-N_{l-1}-1)m+i}\to Z_{q}(t).

These together yield
\int_{0}^{1}[z+Z_{q}(t)]^{2}{\,\rm d}t

as shown in Eqs. (
1.37
) and (
1.1
). For the
m\to\infty
limit of (
4.2
) first we rewrite it as

\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}=\frac{1}{n_{l}}\sum_{q\in C_{l}}\left[-\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{q}_{j}}\left(q-N_{l-1}-1+\frac{i^{q}_{j,r}}{m}\right)\,z^{q}_{j}(i^{q}_{j,r})+\sum_{k=q+1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{q}}\left(q-N_{l-1}-1+\frac{i^{k}_{q,r}}{m}\right)\,z^{k}_{q}(i^{k}_{q,r})\right].

(4.57)

Interchanging the order of summations with respect to
q
and
j
and to
q
and
k
and letting
m
go to infinity one obtains the second form of
\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
, see Eq. (
2
). In particular,

\int_{0}^{1}Z_{q}(t){\,\rm d}t=-\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{q}_{j}}\left(q-N_{l-1}-1+t^{q}_{j,r}\right)\,z^{q}_{j,r}+\sum_{k=q+1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{q}}\left(q-N_{l-1}-1+t^{k}_{q,r}\right)\,z^{k}_{q,r}.

(4.58)

The
m\to\infty
limit of (
4.37
) can be computed with the help of (
4.33
). The lengthy formulas can be found in [Su12]. We will need only
\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}=\overline{\left(Z^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}/L^{2}
and present it in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The last point to check is that the
m\to\infty
limit could indeed be taken under the summation signs. The cycle-dependent part of the summand of
G_{m}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]
,

f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}\left(\{t^{k}_{j,,r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}},\{z^{k}_{j,r}\}_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}\right)=\prod_{l=1}^{p}\delta_{Z^{l}_{1},0}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left[\overline{\left(Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right\}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z+\overline{Z^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)^{2}\right\}

(4.59)

is bounded as

|f_{\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]}|\leq\prod_{l=1}^{p}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}z^{2}\right\}.

(4.60)

Therefore, in
G_{m}\left[\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]
for each pair
j<k
we can apply the dominated convergence theorem to the sum over
\alpha^{k}_{j}
.

4.5
Merger graphs: Analysis of the constraint

We start with a class of graphs slightly different from the one presented under Remark
4
of Section
3
but interesting in its own right. A graph of
V
vertices and
E
edges in this class corresponds to a system of
V
homogeneous linear equations for
E
variables, each appearing with coefficient 1, that has a solution in which all the variables take a nonzero integer value.

Definition 4.1
.

1. A (merger) generator is a circle of even length, two odd circles with a common vertex, or two odd circles joining through a vertex the opposite endpoints of a linear graph. 2. The generators are mergers; merging two mergers through one or more vertices and/or along one or more edges provides a merger. A graph composed of two disconnected mergers is a merger. 3. Given a merger, a set of generators whose merging provides the graph is called a covering. A covering is minimal if each of its elements contributes to the merger with at least one edge not covered by the other generators. A max-min covering is a minimal covering that contains the largest number of generators.

As an example, the Petersen graph is a merger obtained by merging five ”washtub” hexagons. The external edges are covered by three, the middle ones by two, the internal edges by one of the hexagons. Bipartite graphs, suitable subgraphs of the triangular lattice, complete graphs of more than three vertices are also mergers. The definition extends to multigraphs whose construction then involves also two-circles. In general the max-min covering is not unique, but the number of its constituting generators is uniquely determined because of the maximal property.

Proposition 4.1
.

A graph is a merger if and only if to every edge one can assign a nonzero number in such a way that at every vertex the sum of the numbers assigned to the incident edges is zero. The numbers as variables form a manifold whose dimension is greater than or equal to the number of generators in the max-min coverings.

Proof.
To mark the edges of a generator one can use a single and only a single variable denoted by
x_{i}
for the
i
th generator of a merger. To the edges of a circle of length
2n
one assigns
x_{i}
and
-x_{i}
in alternation. The same can be done with two odd circles sharing a vertex. In the case of two odd circles linked by a linear graph one assigns
x_{i}
to those two edges of one of the circles that join the vertex of degree 3, and
-x_{i}
and
x_{i}
in alternation to the remaining edges of the same circle.
One then continues by alternating
-2x_{i}
and
2x_{i}
on the edges of the linear graph until reaching the second circle whose edges can again be marked by
x_{i}
and
-x_{i}
in a proper alternation. When merging, the generators carry their numbers. The number on a multiply covered edge is the sum of the numbers of the covering edges while one keeps the original number for the singly covered edges. Changing some
x_{i}
in case of an accidental cancellation the sums on the edges are nonzero and the constraint remains satisfied.
The number of free variables cannot be smaller than the number of elements in max-min coverings, but it can be larger.
By construction, if the free variables are chosen to be integers, the others also take integers values.
