In the opposite direction the proof goes by noting first that a graph does not contain any merger generator as a subgraph if and only if each of its maximal connected components is without circles or contains a single circle of odd length. The edges of such graphs cannot be marked in the required manner because either they have a vertex of degree 1 or they are unions of disjoint odd circles. Let
\cal G
be any graph with properly marked edges. Thus, it has subgraphs which are merger generators. We can proceed by successive demerging. Let us choose a generator
g
in
\cal G
and select one of its edges denoted by
e
. Let
x
be the number assigned to
e
. Prepare an image
g^{\prime}
of
g
outside
\cal G
and assign
-x
to the image
e^{\prime}
of
e
. This uniquely determines the numbering of the other edges of
g^{\prime}
in such a way that the constraint is satisfied. Add the number on every edge of
g^{\prime}
to the number on its pre-image in
g
. As a result, the new number on
e
is zero. Dropping all the edges from
\cal G
whose new number is zero
and dropping also the vertices that become isolated we obtain a new graph having at least one edge less than
\cal G
while the total numbering still satisfies the constraint. In a finite number of steps we can empty
\cal G
which is, therefore, a merger.
\Box

Now we define the class of graphs that we need for this paper. The vertices of a graph in this class correspond to permutation cycles, its edges represent the nonzero
\alpha^{k}_{j}
that allow the solution of all the equations
Z^{l}_{1}=0
, cf. (
1.40
), with each variable taking a nonzero integer vector value.
We shall refer to these graphs as mergers generated by circles. The forthcoming discussion is restricted to them.

Definition 4.2
.

1. A (merger) generator is a circle of any (even or odd) length
n\geq 2
with
n
different positive integers assigned to the vertices in an arbitrary order. 2. The generators are mergers. Merging two mergers through all their vertices that carry the same number and optionally along some of the edges whose endpoints are common in the two mergers provides a merger. A graph composed of two disconnected mergers with disjoint vertex-numbering is a merger. 3. Given a merger, a set of generators whose merging provides the graph is called a covering. A covering is minimal if each of its element contributes to the merger with at least one edge not covered by the other generators. A max-min covering is a minimal covering that contains the largest number of generators.

Proposition 4.2
.

A graph whose vertices
\{1,2,\dots\}
carry different positive integers
l_{1},l_{2},\dots
is a merger if and only if to every edge one can assign a nonzero vector in such a way that at any vertex
i
the sum of the vectors on the incident edges
(i,j)
taken with minus sign if
l_{j}<l_{i}
and with plus sign if
l_{j}>l_{i}
, is zero.
If
N_{I}
and
M
denote the number of linearly independent vectors and the number of generators in the max-min coverings, respectively, then
N_{I}\geq M
.

In Proposition
4.3
we shall give the precise value of
N_{I}
.

Proof.
First let us see how to assign a vector to the edges of a generator. Let
l_{i}
be the number carried by the
i
th vertex of a
n
-circle, where
i=1,\dots,n
label the clockwise consecutive vertices. Let
x_{12},x_{23},\dots,x_{n-1,n},x_{n1}
denote the
n
edge variables that must assume a suitable value. The equation to be solved at vertex
i
(
n+1\equiv 1
) is one of

(1)\ \ x_{i-1,i}+x_{i,i+1}=0\quad\mbox{if $l_{i-1},l_{i+1}>l_{i}$},\quad(2)\ \ -x_{i-1,i}+x_{i,i+1}=0\quad\mbox{if $l_{i-1}<l_{i}<l_{i+1}$},

(3)\ \ x_{i-1,i}-x_{i,i+1}=0\quad\mbox{if $l_{i-1}>l_{i}>l_{i+1}$},\quad(4)\ \ -x_{i-1,i}-x_{i,i+1}=0\quad\mbox{if $l_{i-1},l_{i+1}<l_{i}$}.

It is seen that whatever be the choice of, say,
x_{12}
, the other variables must take the same value with plus or minus sign. So the solution, if any, is a one-dimensional manifold.
To be definite, let
l_{1}
be the smallest number. Take an arbitrary nonzero vector
v
and set
x_{12}=v
. The equation at vertex 1 is (1), it is solved with
x_{n1}=-v
. We must prove that going around the circle there is no ”frustration”, all the equations can be solved. Call
i
a source if
l_{i}<l_{i-1},l_{i+1}
and a sink if
l_{i}>l_{i-1},l_{i+1}
. It is helpful to imagine an arrow on every edge, pointing towards the larger-numbered vertex. The problem is soluble because the number of sinks equals the number of sources and there is at least one source. Passing a source
-v
changes to
v
while solving equation (1), stays
v
until the next sink and solves equations of the type (2), passing the sink it changes to
-v
while solving equation (4), stays
-v
and solves equations of the type (3), and so on.
By merging the circles of a covering as written down in Proposition
4.1
provides the solution for the merger graph.

The proof in the opposite direction goes again by successive demerging as described above provided we can show that no other vertex-numbered graph than those defined as mergers can be edge-marked in the required manner. Suppose there is such a finite graph. It must contain at least one circle, since linear graphs, tree graphs obviously cannot satisfy the condition at vertices of degree 1. Demerging successively all the circles, what remains is nonempty and cannot be marked – a contradiction.
\Box

As a matter of fact, the numbering can be dropped from the definition because the vertices of any merger of circular graphs can be labelled
a posteriori
and in an arbitrary order with different
l_{1},l_{2},\dots
, still a proper assignment of nonzero vectors to the edges is possible.

Example 1.
A multigraph of two vertices and
n>1
edges is a merger of
n-1
two-circles. Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be the edge variables. The equations at the two vertices are
\pm(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n})=0
. Their general solution with nonzero vectors is
x_{1}=v_{1}
,
x_{2}=-v_{1}+v_{2}
,…,
x_{n-1}=-v_{n-2}+v_{n-1}
,
x_{n}=-v_{n-1}
, a
(n-1)
-dimensional manifold. On the other hand, for
n
even the collection of every second 2-circles constitutes a max-min covering. For
n
odd we need one more circle. So
N_{I}=n-1
and
M=n/2
or
(n+1)/2
.

Example 2.
Consider the complete 4-graph of vertices 1, 2, 3, 4 with
l_{i}=i
. A max-min covering is for example the three circles
(123)
,
(234)
and
(134)
. If prior to merging

\displaystyle z^{2}_{1}=x,\quad z^{3}_{1}=-x,\quad z^{3}_{2}=x,\quad\mbox{for $(123)$}

\displaystyle z^{3}_{2}=y,\quad z^{4}_{2}=-y,\quad z^{4}_{3}=y\quad\mbox{for $(234)$}

\displaystyle z^{3}_{1}=z,\quad z^{4}_{1}=-z,\quad z^{4}_{3}=z\quad\mbox{for $(134)$}

then after merging the edges of the tetrahedron will carry

z^{2}_{1}=x,\quad z^{3}_{1}=-x+z,\quad z^{3}_{2}=x+y,\quad z^{4}_{1}=-z,\quad z^{4}_{2}=-y,\quad z^{4}_{3}=y+z\\

which solve the four equations

z^{2}_{1}+z^{3}_{1}+z^{4}_{1}=0,\quad-z^{2}_{1}+z^{3}_{2}+z^{4}_{2}=0,\quad-z^{3}_{1}-z^{3}_{2}+z^{4}_{3}=0,\quad-z^{4}_{1}-z^{4}_{2}-z^{4}_{3}=0.

A covering which is minimal but not max-min is the two 4-circles
(1234)
,
(1243)
. Using them we would get only two independent variables. A covering which is not minimal is obtained by merging e.g.
(1234)
to the above three triangles with a fourth variable
v
. This only changes
x
to
x^{\prime}=x+v
and
z
to
z^{\prime}=z+v
without increasing the number of free variables. Now
N_{I}=M
.

Example 3.
Consider a part of the triangular lattice composed of four small triangles, three with top up and one in the middle with top down. Let the V=6 vertices be numbered from 1 to 6 along the big outer triangle. This is a merger graph in both senses, generated by
M=3
elements in a max-min covering: according to Definition
4.1
, by the rhombi
(1246)
,
(2346)
and
(2456)
, and according to Definition
4.2
, by the triangles
(126)
,
(234)
and
(456)
.
We focus on mergers of the second kind.
Let the E=9 edge variables form the vector
Z
with
Z=(z^{2}_{1}\,z^{3}_{2}\,z^{4}_{3}\,z^{5}_{4}\,z^{6}_{5}\,z^{6}_{1}\,z^{4}_{2}\,z^{6}_{2}\,z^{6}_{4})^{T}
and let
A
be the
V\times E
matrix of the coefficients in the system of equations
AZ=0
(
A
is the incidence matrix of the directed graph with arrows towards the larger-numbered vertices).
One can check that
{\rm rank}A=5
, so 5 variables take a unique value once for the
N_{I}=4
free variables a nonzero value is chosen. Thus,
N_{I}>M
. This can also be seen by choosing first a value for the variables associated with the three generators, as it was done in the proof of the propositions above. For the triangles, let
z^{2}_{1}=z^{6}_{2}=-z^{6}_{1}=x
,
z^{3}_{2}=z^{4}_{3}=-z^{4}_{2}=y
,
z^{5}_{4}=z^{6}_{5}=-z^{6}_{4}=z
. We can add any
v
to
z^{4}_{2}
so that the new value is
-y+v
; if we change simultaneously
z^{6}_{2}
into
x-v
and
z^{6}_{4}
into
-z+v
, the four vectors
x
,
-y+v
,
x-v
and
-z+v
are linearly independent and
the three equations at vertices 2, 4 and 6, namely

-z^{2}_{1}+z^{3}_{2}+z^{4}_{2}+z^{6}_{2}=0,\qquad-z^{4}_{2}-z^{4}_{3}+z^{5}_{4}+z^{6}_{4}=0,\qquad-z^{6}_{1}-z^{6}_{2}-z^{6}_{4}-z^{6}_{5}=0

remain satisfied. The equations at the 2-degree vertices 1, 3 and 5 are unchanged.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\cal G
be a connected merger of circles having
V
vertices and
E
edges. Let
K
denote the maximal number of linearly independent equations among the
V
equations for the edge variables. Then
K=V-1
independently of
E
, so the number of free variables is
N_{I}=E-V+1
. Moreover, any
V-1
equations are linearly independent.

Proof.
We proceed by induction according to the number of vertices. For
V=2
the claim holds true, see Example 1. Suppose that it holds for
V=n
and let
A_{n}
denote the matrix of the coefficients in the
n
equations. By the induction hypothesis
K\equiv{\rm rank}A_{n}=n-1
. If the vertex
n+1
is coupled to any vertex
i\leq n
with at most a single edge then

A_{n+1}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{n}&{\rm diag}\left(b_{1}^{n+1},\ldots,b_{n}^{n+1}\right)\\
\phantom{.}&\phantom{.}\\
0&-b_{1}^{n+1}\ \ldots\ -b_{n}^{n+1}\end{pmatrix}

where

b_{i}^{n+1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if there is an edge between vertices $i$ and $n+1$}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

and at least two of them is 1. In the general case when vertex
n+1
is coupled to vertex
i
with
r_{i}
edges, the diagonal matrix is replaced by a block-diagonal one where the
i
th block is

B_{i}=(b^{n+1}_{i,1}\ldots b^{n+1}_{i,r_{i}}),\qquad i=1,\dots,n.

At the same time in the last line we have
-b^{n+1}_{1,1}\ \ldots\ -b^{n+1}_{n,r_{n}}
with at least two nonzero elements.
Now in
A_{n+1}
the first
n
lines are linearly independent, and replacing any one with the last line the
n
lines are still linearly independent. On the other hand, the sum of the
n+1
lines gives zero. So
{\rm rank}A_{n+1}=n
.
\quad\Box

Example 4.

For plane graphs and graphs consisting of the vertices and edges of convex polyhedra
N_{I}
is related to the Euler characteristic
V+F-E=2
. Comparison with it shows that
N_{I}=F-1
where
F
is the number of faces (the exterior face included for plane graphs). Example 2 is a tetrahedron with
N_{I}=F-1=3=M
. In the case of Example 3 the general solution can also be obtained by choosing freely a variable for all the four triangles and then merge them.
The procedure applies to any connected plane graph: demerge it into faces, assign to the edges of the circle around each interior face a value as was shown in the proof of Proposition
4.2
, and merge the faces. This is an alternative way to solve the associated system of equations.
For edge-connected plane graphs
M<N_{I}
because to cover the edges joining a vertex of even degree greater than 2 half of the faces incident on that vertex is enough. For the square lattice
M/N_{I}\to 1/2
. If the degree is odd, more faces are necessary. For the honeycomb lattice
M/N_{I}\to 3/4
.

Example 5.
There is another set of circle-generated merger graphs for which the general solution of the system of equations is easily obtained: the complete
n
-graphs. In this case
N_{I}=n(n-1)/2-n+1=(n-1)(n-2)/2
, the number of edges of the complete
(n-1)
-graph. The variables of this latter,
z^{k}_{j}
for
1\leq j<k\leq n-1
are free; then with

z^{n}_{l}=\sum_{j=1}^{l-1}z^{l}_{j}-\sum_{k=l+1}^{n-1}z^{k}_{l}\qquad l=1,\dots,n-1

all the equations will be satisfied. (For the complete 4-graph this offers the alternative to choose first
z^{2}_{1}=x
,
z^{3}_{2}=y
,
z^{3}_{1}=z
and conclude with
z^{4}_{1}=-x-z
,
z^{4}_{2}=x-y
,
z^{4}_{3}=y+z
.) Note that for the complete
n
-graphs
N_{I}=M
: the triangles
(j,k,n)
with
j<k<n
form a max-min covering and
M=(n-1)(n-2)/2
indeed. The edges
(j,k)
are covered by a single triangle and each edge
(l,n)
is covered by
n-2
triangles.

In general, finding
N_{I}
edges that can carry free variables or, equivalently, a unimodular
(V-1)\times(V-1)
submatrix of the incidence matrix (whose columns belong to a possible set of dependent variables) may be difficult. Instead, one can easily find
N_{I}
circles that constitute a covering, and assign a free variable to each circle. The variable is then equipped with edge-dependent signs on the edges of the circle. After merging the
N_{I}
circles, on every edge we obtain the sum of the signed circle-variables belonging to the circles incident on that edge, and the linear equations at each vertex will be satisfied with nonzero integer vectors.

5
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Introduce

\tilde{G}\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x)=\exp\left\{\frac{\beta\hat{u}(0)N(N-1)}{2L^{d}}\right\}G\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x),\quad\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)=\exp\left\{\frac{\beta\hat{u}(0)N(N-1)}{2L^{d}}\right\}G^{N}_{n}(x)

(5.1)

and substitute in the first formula the asymptotic form Eq. (
3.10
). This yields (
n_{0}=n
)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x)=\sum_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}|1\leq j<k\leq N\}}\Delta_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\},\{n_{l}\}_{0}^{p}}\ \left(L^{-d}\right)^{K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}}\prod_{1\leq j<k\leq N}\frac{\left(-\beta\right)^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}}{\alpha^{k}_{j}!}\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}\int_{0}^{1}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,r}\int{\,\rm d}x^{k}_{j,r}\ \hat{u}\left(x^{k}_{j,r}\right)

\displaystyle\left[\delta(X^{0}_{1},\dots,X^{p}_{1})\exp\left\{-\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right\}f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\prod_{l=1}^{p}\exp\left\{-\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left[\overline{\left(X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right\}f_{n_{l}}\left(0;L\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\right]

where

f_{n_{l}}\left(0;L\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)=\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}(z+L\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}})^{2}\right\}

(5.3)

and

\displaystyle f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}(z+L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}})^{2}\right\}\cos\left[\frac{2\pi}{L}z\cdot x\right]

(5.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{L^{d}}{\lambda_{n\beta}^{d}}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi(x+Lz)^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}\cos\left[2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot(x+Lz)\right].

Equations (
1.32
) can be replaced with

\langle x|\sigma^{N,L}_{1}|0\rangle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\,\frac{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)}{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)},\qquad\rho^{N,L}_{0}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\,\frac{\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\,\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)}{L^{d}\,\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)}.

(5.5)

Let us start by proving a lemma. The first form of
f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)
suggests the evaluation

f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\sim L^{d}\int\exp\left\{-\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}(y+\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}})^{2}\right\}\cos 2\pi y\cdot x{\,\rm d}y=\frac{L^{d}}{\lambda_{n\beta}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x.

However, this gives the right result only when
n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}/L^{2}\to 0
. To cover all the cases when
n
tends to infinity we need detailed estimates.

Lemma 5.1
.

For
x
fixed, when
N,L\to\infty
,
N/L^{d}=\rho>0
we have the following asymptotic forms for
f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)
.

(i)

If
n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}/L^{2}\to 0
then

f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)=\frac{L^{d}}{\lambda_{n\beta}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}\left[\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x+o(1)\right].

(5.6)

(ii)

If
n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}/L^{2}\to\infty
then

\displaystyle f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\}^{2}\right\}

(5.7)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\left[\left(1+\sum_{z:\,\max|z_{i}|=1}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}z\cdot(z+2\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\})\right\}\right)\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x+o(1)\right]

where

\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\}
is the fractional part of
L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
, each component of which is bounded in modulus by 1/2.

(iii)

If
n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}/L^{2}=c>0
then

f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)=\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\pi c(z+\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\})^{2}\right\}\left[\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x+o(1)\right].

(5.8)

Proof.
(i) From the second form of
f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)
,

\displaystyle f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{L^{d}}{\lambda_{n\beta}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\left[\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x+\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi\left[(x+Lz)^{2}-x^{2}\right]}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot(x+Lz)\right].

Because for
z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}

|x+Lz|^{2}-x^{2}\geq L^{2}z^{2}\left(1-2|x|/L\right),

the sum over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
can be bounded above in modulus by

\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi L^{2}z^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}(1-2|x|/L)\right\}

which tends to zero as
L^{2}/(n\lambda_{\beta}^{2})
goes to infinity.

(ii) We use the first form of
f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)
. With
L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}=[L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}]+\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\}
, a decomposition into integer and fractional parts,

\displaystyle f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)=\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}(z+\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\})^{2}\right\}\cos\frac{2\pi}{L}\left(z-[L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}]\right)\cdot x\hskip 156.49014pt

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\}^{2}\right\}\left[\cos\frac{2\pi}{L}[L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}]\cdot x+\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}z\cdot(z+2\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\})\right\}\cos\frac{2\pi}{L}\left(z-[L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}]\right)\cdot x\right].

z\cdot(z+2\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\})\geq 0
, and the sum restricted to
\max_{1\leq i\leq d}|z_{i}|\geq 2
is
o(1)
. The sum over
z
with
\max|z_{i}|=1
can give a contribution of order 1 if
|\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\}_{i}|=1/2
for one or more components of
\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\}
. For these
z

\cos\frac{2\pi}{L}\left(z-[L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}]\right)\cdot x=\cos\frac{2\pi}{L}\left(L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}-\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\}-z\right)\cdot x=\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x+O(L^{-1}).

However,
|\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\}_{i}|=1/2
can only be if the times
t^{k}_{j,r}
entering
L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
(only for
j\leq n
,
k>n
, cf. Eq. (
2
))
take value from a zero-measure subset of
[0,1]^{M}
where
M=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=n+1}^{N}\alpha^{k}_{j}
. Because
f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)
appears in
\tilde{G}\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x)
under integrals over
t^{k}_{j,r}
, the sum over
z
with
\max|z_{i}|=1
can be dropped.

(iii) Looking at the first line of (
5
) it is seen that the summand has a summable majorizing function. Therefore the asymptotic approximation can be done under the summation sign: for any
z
fixed,

\cos\frac{2\pi}{L}\left(z-[L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}]\right)\cdot x=\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x+O(L^{-1})
.

\Box

The essential information provided by the above lemma is that for large systems the
x
-dependence of
f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)
and, hence, of
\tilde{G}\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x)
is in the factors

\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}
and
\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x
. From Eqs. (
5.6
)-(
5.8
)

f_{n}\left(0;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\frac{L^{d}}{\lambda_{n\beta}^{d}}\,[1+o(1)]&\mbox{if}&n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}/L^{2}\to 0\\
e^{-\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\}^{2}/L^{2}}\left[1+o(1)\right]&\mbox{if}&n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}/L^{2}\to\infty\\
\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}e^{-\pi c(z+\{L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\})^{2}}&\mbox{if}&n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}/L^{2}=c.\end{array}\right.

(5.11)

Comparison with (
5.6
)-(
5.8
) shows that

f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}f_{n}\left(0;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}\left[\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x+o(1)\right]&\mbox{if}&n=O(1)\\
f_{n}\left(0;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\left[\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x+o(1)\right]&\mbox{if}&n\to\infty.\\
\end{array}\right.

However, because
\exp\{-\pi x^{2}/(n\lambda_{\beta}^{2})\}\to 1
as
n\to\infty
,

f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)=f_{n}\left(0;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}\left[\cos 2\pi\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\cdot x+o(1)\right]

(5.12)

holds true in all the cases. This can be substituted into Eq. (
5
).

To write down
\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)/\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)
we separate the contribution of the
p
closed trajectories,

\displaystyle H_{N-n}=\sum_{p=1}^{N-n}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\geq 1:\sum_{1}^{p}n_{l}=N-n}\frac{1}{\prod_{1}^{p}n_{l}}\hskip 256.0748pt

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}|n+1\leq j<k\leq N\}}\left(\prod_{n+1\leq j<k\leq N}\frac{\left(-\beta\right)^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}}{\alpha^{k}_{j}!}\right)\int_{0}^{1}\prod_{n+1\leq j<k\leq N}\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,r}\int\prod_{n+1\leq j<k\leq N}\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\,\rm d}x^{k}_{j,r}\hat{u}\left(x^{k}_{j,r}\right)

\displaystyle\left[\delta(X^{0}_{1},\dots,X^{p}_{1})\,\Delta_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\},\{n_{l}\}_{0}^{p}}\,\left(L^{-d}\right)^{K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}}\prod_{l=1}^{p}e^{-\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left[\overline{\left(X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]}f_{n_{l}}\left(0;L\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\right].

H_{N-n}
depends on all the variables that connect the cycles
l=1,\dots,p
to cycle 0, i.e. on

\{\alpha^{k}_{j},x^{k}_{j,r},t^{k}_{j,r}|j\leq n,k\geq n+1,r\leq\alpha^{k}_{j}\}.

Let

\displaystyle\Theta^{N}_{n}(y)=\sum_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}|1\leq j\leq n,\,j+1\leq k\leq N\}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{k=j+1}^{N}\frac{\left(-\beta\right)^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}}{\alpha^{k}_{j}!}\right)\int_{0}^{1}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{k=j+1}^{N}\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,r}

(5.14)

\displaystyle\int\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{k=j+1}^{N}\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\,\rm d}x^{k}_{j,r}\,\hat{u}\left(x^{k}_{j,r}\right)H_{N-n}\exp\left\{-\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-y^{2}\right]\right\}f_{n}\left(0;Ly\right)\delta(y-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}),

i.e. the integrations over
\{t^{k}_{j,r},\,x^{k}_{j,r}|j\leq n,k\geq j+1,r\leq\alpha^{k}_{j}\}
are restricted to values that yield
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}=y
.
Then

\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)=\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Theta^{N}_{n}(y)[\cos(2\pi y\cdot x)+o(1)]{\,\rm d}y.

(5.15)

Introducing

\nu^{N,L}_{n}(y)=\frac{\Theta^{N}_{n}(y)}{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)}=\frac{\Theta^{N}_{n}(y)}{\int\Theta^{N}_{n}(y^{\prime}){\,\rm d}y^{\prime}},

(5.16)

the ratio
\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)/\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)
takes the form

\frac{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)}{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)}=\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}[\cos(2\pi y\cdot x)+o(1)]\,\nu^{N,L}_{n}(y){\,\rm d}y\,.

(5.17)

\int\nu^{N,L}_{n}(y){\,\rm d}y=1
, but
\nu^{N,L}_{n}(y)
can be negative for some values of
y
. Note also that
\nu^{N,L}_{n}(y)=\nu^{N,L}_{n}(-y)
and, if
u
is spherically symmetric then
\nu^{N,L}_{n}
has a cubic symmetry that tends to spherical as
L
inreases.

5.1
Condition for ODLRO

The general concept of off-diagonal long-range order was introduced by C. N. Yang [Y]. In the present context it means that

\sigma_{1}(x)
does not go to zero as
x
goes to infinity.
Now with

\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\frac{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)}{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)}\leq\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}\leq 1

(5.18)

and

\rho-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}=\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{n=M+1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}

(5.19)

one obtains

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\frac{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)}{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)}+\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{n=M+1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\frac{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)}{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)}

(5.20)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}+\rho-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}.

This is the upper bound in (
1.24
).
If in the infinite system the full density arises from particles in finite cycles, i.e.
\rho=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}
, then

\sigma_{1}(x)\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\},

(5.21)

the infinite sum decays to zero as
x
goes to infinity. Thus, there is no ODLRO and no BEC either:

\rho^{N,L}_{0}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\frac{1}{L^{d}}\int_{\Lambda}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}{\,\rm d}x\rightarrow 0.

(5.22)

In the absence of interaction the inequalities hold with equality.

On the other hand, if
\rho-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}>0
,
the infinite-volume Gibbs measure assigns a positive weight to infinite cycles. When
N
,
L
and
n
tend to infinity then for
x
fixed
\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi x^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}
tends to 1, and

\frac{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)}{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)}\to\Phi(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\cos(2\pi y\cdot x)\nu({\,\rm d}y).

(5.23)

For the existence of ODLRO
\nu
must contain a positive multiple of
\delta_{0}
:
\Phi
being nonnegative, in the absence of an atomic component at 0 there could be no other atomic component either, so
\nu
would define a continuous measure, and by the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma
\Phi(x)
would go to zero when
x
goes to infinity.
For the ideal Bose gas it is easy to check that
G^{N}_{n}(x)/G^{N}_{n}(0)=f_{n}(x;0)/f_{n}(0;0)\to 1
whenever
n\to\infty
, i.e.
\nu=\delta_{0}
.
In principle,
\nu
and
\Phi
may depend on how fast
n
increases with
N
. It will be seen that
\nu^{N,L}_{n}
converges weakly to
\delta_{0}
by concentrating to a
1/\sqrt{n}
-neighborhood of the origin, so the rate of convergence depends only on
n
.

Let now
g_{N}<N
be any monotone increasing sequence that tends to infinity.
Clearly,

\sigma_{1}(x)\geq\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{n=g_{N}}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\frac{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)}{\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)}.

For
N
large enough,
n\geq g_{N}
and
y
in a
1/\sqrt{n}
-neighborhood of the origin
\cos(2\pi y\cdot x)\geq 1-x^{2}/(g_{N}\lambda^{2})
with some constant
\lambda^{2}
, therefore

\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x)/\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)\geq\exp\{-\pi x^{2}/(g_{N}\lambda_{\beta}^{2})\}(1-x^{2}/(g_{N}\lambda^{2})),

implying

\sigma_{1}(x)\geq\lim_{N,L\to\infty,N/L^{d}=\rho}\sum_{n=g_{N}}^{N}\rho^{N,L}_{n}.

Because this holds true for any
g_{N}\to\infty
, we obtain the lower bound

\sigma_{1}(x)\geq\rho-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho^{N,L}_{n}.

(5.24)

To see the concentration of
\nu^{N,L}_{n}
we must analyse the large-
n
behavior of
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
and
\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}
. From Eq. (
2
) for
l=0

n\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}=-\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(k-j)\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}x^{k}_{j,r}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{N-n}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\left(j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}\right)x^{+k}_{j,r}=:nY^{0}_{0}+nY^{+}_{0}.

(5.25)

In the new notation we restart the count of the particles outside the zeroth cycle. Now cycle 0 is distinguished without making distinction among the different partitions of
N-n
. Similarly, combining Eq. (
4.37
) with (
4.33
) and taking the
m\to\infty
limit we find, cf. [Su12],

\displaystyle n\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq n}\ \sum_{1\leq j^{\prime}<k^{\prime}\leq n}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}\sum_{r^{\prime}=1}^{\alpha^{k^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}}A^{k^{\prime}j^{\prime}r^{\prime}}_{kjr}x^{k}_{j,r}\cdot x^{k^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime},r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{N-n}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{N-n}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\sum_{r^{\prime}=1}^{\alpha^{+k^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}}A^{+k^{\prime}j^{\prime}r^{\prime}}_{+kjr}x^{+k}_{j,r}\cdot x^{+k^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime},r^{\prime}}-2\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq n}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{N-n}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}\sum_{r^{\prime}=1}^{\alpha^{+k^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}}A^{+k^{\prime}j^{\prime}r^{\prime}}_{kjr}x^{k}_{j,r}\cdot x^{+k^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime},r^{\prime}}

where

A^{+k^{\prime}j^{\prime}r^{\prime}}_{+kjr}=\min\left\{j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r},\,j^{\prime}-1+t^{+k^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime},r^{\prime}}\right\},

(5.27)

A^{+k^{\prime}j^{\prime}r^{\prime}}_{kjr}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}k-j&\mbox{if}&k<j^{\prime}\\
j^{\prime}-j+t^{+k^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime},r^{\prime}}-t^{k}_{j,r}&\mbox{if}&j<j^{\prime}<k\\
0&\mbox{if}&j^{\prime}<j\ ,\end{array}\right.

(5.28)

A^{k^{\prime}j^{\prime}r^{\prime}}_{kjr}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}0&\mbox{if}&j^{\prime}<k^{\prime}<j<k\ \ \mbox{or}\ \ j<k<j^{\prime}<k^{\prime}\\
k-j&\mbox{if}&j^{\prime}<j<k<k^{\prime}\\
k^{\prime}-j^{\prime}&\mbox{if}&j<j^{\prime}<k^{\prime}<k\\
k-j^{\prime}+t^{k}_{j,r}-t^{k^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime},r^{\prime}}&\mbox{if}&j<j^{\prime}<k<k^{\prime}\\
k^{\prime}-j+t^{k^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime},r^{\prime}}-t^{k}_{j,r}&\mbox{if}&j^{\prime}<j<k^{\prime}<k\ .\\
\end{array}\right.

(5.29)

If two indices coincide, there may appear a correction in modulus not larger than 1, coming from the times
t^{k}_{j,r},t^{+k}_{j,r}
, but there is no such correction to
A^{kjr}_{kjr}=k-j
.

In the analysis below we treat
n
as an independent variable and
N={\cal N}(n)
strictly increasing through jumps. The fastest increase of
n
that we shall consider here is
n\sim\varepsilon N
with some
0<\varepsilon<1
, so the slowest increase for
{\cal N}
is
{\cal N}(n)=\lfloor n/\varepsilon\rfloor
. Accordingly,
L
must satisfy
{\cal N}(n)/L^{d}=\rho
.

Because
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
and
\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}
depend on all the variables
\{\alpha^{k}_{j},\,t^{k}_{j,r},\,x^{k}_{j,r}|j\leq n,k\geq j+1,r\leq\alpha^{k}_{j}\}
, it is advantageous to rearrange the summations in
\Theta^{N}_{n}
. Let

\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}=\{\alpha^{k}_{j}|1\leq j<k\leq n\},\quad\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}=\{\alpha^{+k}_{j}|1\leq j\leq n,\,1\leq k\leq{\cal N}(n)-n\},\quad\bt@\alpha_{n}=\{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n},\,\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}\}.

(5.30)

Let, moreover,

\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|=\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j\ \alpha^{k}_{k-j},\quad\|\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}\|=\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal N}(n)-n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\ j\alpha^{+k}_{j},\quad\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|=\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|+\|\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}\|.

(5.31)

Then

\Theta^{N}_{n}(y)=\sum_{A,B=0}^{\infty}\,\sum_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}:\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|=A}\,\sum_{\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}:\|\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}\|=B}\vartheta_{\bt@\alpha_{n}}(y)

(5.32)

where
\vartheta_{\bt@\alpha_{n}}(y)
is defined by Eq. (
5.14
).
For most of what follows we shall consider the infinite sequences
\bt@\alpha^{0}=\{\alpha^{k}_{j}|1\leq j<k<\infty\}
and
\bt@\alpha^{+}=\{\alpha^{+k}_{j}|j,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}\}
as given beforehand, and then
\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}
and
\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}
are their restrictions to a finite number of elements as shown in Eq. (
5.30
).
Since
t^{k}_{j,r}
and
t^{+k}_{j,r}
play a minor role, they can also be considered as given beforehand.

\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)=\int\Theta^{N}_{n}(y){\,\rm d}y
written as a sum over
\bt@\alpha_{n}
makes it possible to examine the contribution of the different terms and to decide which of them dominate the sum. In order to better distinguish between the different cases we provisionally strengthen the condition on
\hat{u}
by assuming that its support is compact, implying also
\int\hat{u}(x)x^{2}{\,\rm d}x<\infty
.

(i) If
\bt@\alpha
contains a finite number of nonzero elements then
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|
attains a constant at a finite
n
, implying

\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to 0
. We shall refer to these sequences as rationals,
while those with infinitely many positive elements will be referred to as irrationals.
With all the
x^{k}_{j,r}
and
x^{+k}_{j,r}
on the support of
\hat{u}
(that we suppose in the sequel)
n\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
and
n\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}
are bounded, so
|\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}|=O(1/n)
and

n\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]=n\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-O(1/n)=O(1).

(ii)
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
tends surely to zero also for some irrational sequences.
If
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|\to\infty
but

\frac{\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|}{n}=\frac{1}{n}\left[\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j\ \alpha^{k}_{k-j}+\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal N}(n)-n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\ j\alpha^{+k}_{j}\right]\to 0

(5.33)

then
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
goes to zero, although the decay is slower than
1/n
. An example is
\bt@\alpha^{+}=\bt@0
,
\alpha^{k}_{k-1}=1
if
k
is a prime and
\alpha^{k}_{j}=0
otherwise. Concerning the asymptotic form of
n\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]
more information is necessary and will be obtained later.

(iii) To enlarge further the family of irrational sequences yielding
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to 0
and also to get some insight into the asymptotic form of
\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}
we use probabilistic arguments.
The vectors
x^{k}_{j,r}
and
x^{+k}_{j,r}
can be considered as identically distributed zero-mean random variables with the common probability density
|\hat{u}(\cdot)|/\|\hat{u}\|_{1}
. Viewed as such,
n\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
is the result of a finite number of steps of a symmetric random walk in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
The vectors
x^{k}_{j,r}
are independent among themselves and from
x^{+k}_{j,r}
, but the latter are weakly dependent because their sum vanishes,

X^{0}_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{N-n}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}x^{+k}_{j,r}=0.

(5.34)

One vector, e.g.
x^{+1}_{1,1}
can be expressed with the others, yielding

nY^{+}_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{N-n}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\left(j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right)x^{+k}_{j,r}.

(5.35)

The vectors appearing here with a nonzero coefficient are already independent. The product measures for finite
N,L
form a consistent family and can be extended into a unique probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}
in the measurable space
\left(({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{\infty},\Sigma\right)
, where
\Sigma
is the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing all sets that depend only on a finite number of
x^{k}_{j,r}
,
x^{+k}_{j,r}
. In a short-hand notation
\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)
can be rewritten as

\tilde{G}^{{\cal N}(n)}_{n}(0)=\sum_{\bt@\alpha_{n}}\frac{(-\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1})^{\bt@\alpha_{n}}}{\bt@\alpha_{n}!}\int{\,\rm d}t_{\bt@\alpha_{n}}\,{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{{\cal N}(n)-n}\exp\left\{-\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right\}f_{n}\left(0;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\right].

(5.36)

Now
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to 0

is a tail event, so it occurs with probability 0 or 1.
In the probabilistic setting the convergence of
Y^{0}_{0}
to zero is an instance of the strong law of large numbers and it holds with probability one e.g. for the sequence

\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a&{\rm if}\ j\leq j_{0},k=2,3,\dots\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(5.37)

where
a,j_{0}\geq 1
. This can be seen by expressing
Y^{0}_{0}
as an average of independent zero-mean random variables,

Y^{0}_{0}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=2}^{n}\xi_{k}\quad\mbox{with}\quad\xi_{k}=-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{k-j}}x^{k}_{k-j,r}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathbb{E}}[\xi_{k}^{2}]=\frac{1}{\lambda_{u}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j^{2}\alpha^{k}_{k-j}.

(5.38)

Here the length
\lambda_{u}
is defined by
1/\lambda_{u}^{2}=\int|\hat{u}(x)|x^{2}{\,\rm d}x/\|\hat{u}\|_{1}
.
The condition

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}[\xi_{k}^{2}]<\infty

(5.39)

implies that with probability one
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{-1}\xi_{k}
is convergent and
n^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}\to 0
[Fe], and is satisfied by (
5.38
). So for
\bt@\alpha^{0}
defined by Eq. (
5.37
)
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|\propto n
and
Y^{0}_{0}\to 0
almost surely. The latter is true also if in (
5.37
)

k
is restricted to a positive- or zero-density subset of
{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
. We shall return to this case later. In these examples
\xi_{k}
is a bounded sequence (for
x^{k}_{k-j,r}\in{\rm supp\,}\hat{u}
). The condition (
5.39
) may hold also if in (
5.38
)
\xi_{k}
is unbounded, e.g., if

\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}a&{\rm if}&j\leq k^{\theta},k=2,3,\dots\\
0&{\rm if}&j>k^{\theta}\end{array}\right.

(5.40)

provided that
\theta<1/3
. In this case
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|/n
diverges as
n^{2\theta}
, still
Y^{0}_{0}\to 0
almost surely.

To apply the strong law of large numbers to
Y^{+}_{0}
, we extend the summation with respect to
k
up to infinity and introduce

\eta_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\left(j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right)x^{+k}_{j,r},\quad V_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}.

Then

{\mathbb{E}}[\eta_{j}^{2}]=\frac{1}{\lambda_{u}^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\left(j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right)^{2},

and

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}[\eta_{j}^{2}]=\frac{1}{\lambda_{u}^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\frac{\left(j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right)^{2}}{j^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{u}^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha^{+k}_{j}<\infty

if
\alpha^{+k}_{j}>0
only for a finite number of pairs
(j,k)
, i.e. if
\bt@\alpha^{+}
is rational. This guaranties the sure convergence of
V_{n}
and also of
Y^{+}_{0}
to zero, but the result is not new.

In Eq. (
5.38
) we wrote
Y^{0}_{0}
as
1/n
times the resulting vector of
n
steps of a random walk. If the step lengths are bounded then the averaged distancing from the origin must decay as
1/\sqrt{n}
. The rest of the proof is devoted to show that in the dominant contribution to the partition function the step lengths are indeed bounded.

(iv) More systematically, we can find irrational sequences giving rise to
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to 0
a.s. as follows. Note first that

{\mathbb{P}}(\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to y)=0
if
y\neq 0
:
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to y
is also a tail event, and because
{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}]=0
,
{\mathbb{P}}(\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to y)=1
is impossible .
This implies that for the given
\bt@\alpha
the asymptotic probability distribution is continuous outside 0, but it is not excluded that also
{\mathbb{P}}(\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to 0)=0
, e.g.
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
is symmetrically distributed about 0. On the other hand,
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\to 0
already implies
{\mathbb{P}}(\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to 0)=1
. However, as shown below, arguing with
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\to 0
will not significantly extend the result of points (i)-(iii).

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Y^{0}_{0}\right)^{2}\right]+{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Y^{+}_{0}\right)^{2}\right]

where

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left|Y^{0}_{0}\right|^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{n^{2}\lambda_{u}^{2}}\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j^{2}\alpha^{k}_{k-j},\quad{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left|Y^{+}_{0}\right|^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{n^{2}\lambda_{u}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal N}(n)-n}\ \sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\left(j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right)^{2}.

(5.41)

Hence,
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}
goes to zero with probability one if

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j^{2}\alpha^{k}_{k-j}\to 0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal N}(n)-n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\ \sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\left(j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right)^{2}\to 0.

(5.42)

Equation (
5.42
) can hold if
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|/n
does not go to zero or even tends to infinity. For example, the first condition is satisfied for
\bt@\alpha^{0}
defined by (
5.37
) or (
5.40
); thus, one can reproduce the result obtained from the strong law of large numbers. With (
5.37
),
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|/n\sim 1
but

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Y^{0}_{0}\right)^{2}\right]\propto\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{\min\{k-1,j_{0}\}}j^{2}\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=O\left(1/n\right);

with (
5.40
),
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|/n\sim n^{2\theta}

but

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Y^{0}_{0}\right)^{2}\right]\propto\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{\min\{k-1,k^{\theta}\}}j^{2}\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{1-3\theta}}\right)\to 0

if
\theta<1/3
.

The second condition in (
5.42
) is also fulfilled by some irrational sequences.
Suppose first that
{\cal N}(n)/n^{2}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, for example,
n=\varepsilon N
. If for any
k

\alpha^{+k}_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}a&{\rm if}&j\leq j_{0}\\
0&{\rm if}&j>j_{0}.\end{array}\right.

then

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal N}(n)-n}\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}\ \sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\left(j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{j_{0}(j_{0}+1)(2j_{0}+1)a}{6}\ \frac{{\cal N}(n)-n}{n^{2}}\to 0.

(5.43)

If
{\cal N}(n)/n^{2}
does not go to zero, let
\{k_{i}\}
be a lacunary sequence such that
n^{-2}\sum_{i:\ k_{i}\leq{\cal N}(n)-n}1\to 0
. Then for

\alpha^{+k}_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a&{\rm if}\ j\leq j_{0},\ k\in\{k_{i}\}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.

the condition is satisfied,
Y^{+}_{0}\to 0
with probability one. This is new but irrelevant.
The statistical weight of
\bt@\alpha
– rational or irrational, producing
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to 0
or not – is decreased by a factor
\left(L^{-d}\right)^{K_{\bt@\alpha}}
in which
K_{\bt@\alpha}
(depending only on
\alpha^{k}_{j}
where
j
and
k
are in different cycles) is
at least as large as the number of cycles which the zeroth cycle is coupled with, cf. Proposition
4.3
.
This suggests that the asymptotic contribution of each cycle to
K_{\bt@\alpha}
is finite; or, in graph language, the infinite graph
{\cal G}_{\bt@\alpha}
is almost surely of finite degree, whether or not there exist infinite cycles. Later we shall see that more is true:
\bt@\alpha^{+}
(and its analogues for cycles
1,\dots,p
) must be rational.

(v)
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}})^{2}}\right]\geq{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]
, therefore
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}})^{2}}\right]\to 0
implies
{\mathbb{P}}(\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to 0)=1
, but this will not be new.
Substituting
x^{+1}_{1,1}
from Eq. (
5.34
) into Eq. (
5.1
) and taking the expectation value,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}})^{2}}\right]=\frac{1}{n\lambda_{u}^{2}}\left[\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j\alpha^{k}_{k-j}+\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal N}(n)-n}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\ \sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\left(j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal N}(n)-n}\ \sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{1}}\left(t^{+k}_{1,r}+t^{+1}_{1,1}-2\min\left\{t^{+k}_{1,r},t^{+1}_{1,1}\right\}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\lambda_{u}^{2}}\left[\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j\alpha^{k}_{k-j}+\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal N}(n)-n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\ \sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\left|j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right|\right]\approx\frac{1}{n\lambda_{u}^{2}}\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|.

(5.44)

Thus,
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}})^{2}}\right]\to 0
is equivalent to (
5.33
) which was the condition for
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to 0
surely.

(vi) To summarize, if
\bt@\alpha
is rational, the result is deterministic,
|\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}|=O(1/n)
and
\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}=O(1/n)
. If
\bt@\alpha
is irrational and
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|/n\to 0
, the result is still deterministic, both
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
and
\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}
go surely to zero. We found also other irrational
\bt@\alpha
with non-decaying or even diverging
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|/n

that allow for an
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
almost surely converging to zero. However, as we shall see, while
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|\sim n
provides the leading-order contribution, no
\bt@\alpha
with
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|
diverging faster than
n
contributes asymptotically to
\nu^{N,L}_{n}
.

By inspecting

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{n\lambda_{u}^{2}}\left[\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j\alpha^{k}_{k-j}\left(1-\frac{j}{n}\right)\right.

(5.45)

\displaystyle+\left.\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal N}(n)-n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{r=1}^{\alpha^{+k}_{j}}\left|j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right|\left(1-\frac{\left|j-1+t^{+k}_{j,r}-t^{+1}_{1,1}\right|}{n}\right)\right]

one observes that, unless
\alpha^{k}_{k-j}>0
for
k
and
j
of order
n
,
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]
is of the same order as
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}\right]
.
The exception is illustrated by the following example. Let
\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}=\bt@0
,
k_{0}\geq 1
,
\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=1
for
n-k_{0}\leq j<k\leq n
and
\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=0
otherwise. Then
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|=\sum_{n-k_{0}\leq j<k\leq n}j
is of order
n
, so both
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}\right]
and
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]
are of order 1, but their difference computed from the first line of (
5.45
) is of order
1/n
. Later we return to this example, here we note only that
\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}
is exceptional also because it is not the restriction of some infinite
\bt@\alpha^{0}
, since
\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\to\bt@0
as
n\to\infty
.

Focusing first on the ”regular” case
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}\right]\leq C\left({\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right)
with some
C>1
we find that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{n}{\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]=c<\frac{1}{\lambda_{u}^{2}}={\mathbb{E}}\left[(x^{k}_{j,r})^{2}\right]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{n}{\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}\right],

(5.46)

that is,

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{n}{\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right]\to\lambda_{u}^{-2}-c>0.

(5.47)

To see the dominant contribution to
\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)
one must estimate the weight of all the
\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}
and
\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}
with a given norm. This weight is composed of their number multiplied by
\prod(\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1})^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}/\alpha^{k}_{j}!
, and can compensate the loss due to a decaying

\exp\left\{-\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right\}
.
Consider first
\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}
. As a typical example, let
\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=a\geq 1
for
j=1,\dots,j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}
and for
i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}
different values of
k
(each larger than
j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}
), and
\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=0
otherwise. Then

\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|=\frac{a}{2}i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}(j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}+1)

and the associated weight is

\left[\frac{(\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1})^{a}}{a!}\right]^{i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}{n-j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\choose i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}

whose logarithm

\displaystyle\ln\left\{\left[\frac{(\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1})^{a}}{a!}\right]^{i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}{n-j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\choose i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle a\,i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}(\ln\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1}-\ln a+1)-\frac{1}{2}i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\ln 2\pi a+\ln{n-j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\choose i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}

(5.48)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|\frac{2}{j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}+1}\left(\ln\frac{\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1}}{a}+1-\frac{\ln 2\pi a}{2a}\right)+\ln{n-j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\choose i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}

is to be added to
-\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}n\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]
.

(vii) We start by proving that if
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\propto\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|/n

goes to infinity, the contribution to the partition function is negligible.
Assume that
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|\sim\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|
; we shall see that this is indeed the case, the contribution of
\|\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}\|
to
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|
is finite. For a reference we note that adding

-\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]

to (
5.48
)
yields the averaged exponent

E_{\rm av}=-\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|\left[\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left(\lambda_{u}^{-2}-c\right)-\frac{2}{j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}+1}\left(\ln\frac{\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1}}{a}+1-\frac{\ln 2\pi a}{2a}\right)\right]+\ln{n-j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\choose i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}.

(5.49)

Because
i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\leq n
,
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|/n=a\,i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}(j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}+1)/2n\to\infty
can occur only if

j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}
increases with
n
(
a
should not increase beyond
\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1}
because
(\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1})^{a}/a!
is maximal roughly at this value.)
Then the quantity in the square bracket tends to
\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left(\lambda_{u}^{-2}-c\right)
; therefore, if
n
is large enough,
E_{\rm av}
is a negative multiple of
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|
with a positive

O(n)=o(\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|)
correction coming from the entropy.

Consider now the random exponent

\displaystyle E=-\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|\left[\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left(\frac{n}{\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right)-\frac{2}{j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}+1}\left(\ln\frac{\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1}}{a}+1-\frac{\ln 2\pi a}{2a}\right)\right]+\ln{n-j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\choose i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}

\displaystyle=-\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}n\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]+a\,i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\left(\ln\frac{\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1}}{a}+1-\frac{\ln 2\pi a}{2a}\right)+\ln{n-j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\choose i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}.

(5.50)

Assume first that there is no concentration of probability at zero, i.e.

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{n}{\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]>0\right)=1.

(5.51)

Then, due to
j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\to\infty
, the
quantity
in the square bracket in the middle member of (
5.1
) becomes positive and the random exponent also tends to minus infinity as
n
increases.
Would (
5.51
) fail,
\bt@\alpha
with
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|/n\to\infty
would still be
asymptotically
irrelevant. If in Eq. (
5.1
)
E<0
or
E=o(n)
positive, the contribution is negligible compared to that of other
\bt@\alpha
’s yielding
E\propto n
, see below.
E
cannot be positive and increase faster than
n
because that would be incompatible with a stable pair potential.
It remains the possibility that
i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\sim n
, but then
i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}=o(n)
and therefore the entropy is also
o(n)
. If
\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}
could be of order one with a non-vanishing probability then for
\beta
large enough
E
would tend to minus infinity. At last, suppose that the only term of order
n
is the middle one. This is independent of the variables
\{x^{k}_{j,r},\,x^{+k}_{j,r}\}
and, hence, of the pair interactions. With the choice
a=\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1}
and supposing
n\propto N
, the only relevant case (or counting with a similar contribution from the other cycles), a multiple of
\|\hat{u}\|_{1}=u(0)
would be subtracted from the free energy per particle and, in effect, from the ground state energy per particle. This, however, is in contradiction with some earlier results [Li7], [Su5] saying that for positive type pair potentials at asymptotically high densities the ground state energy per particle is
\rho\hat{u}(0)/2
. This is exactly the quantity in the exponential factor that appears in
G[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}](0)
and in the partition function.
\rho\hat{u}(0)/2
is the mean-field result, at
\beta<\infty
it overshoots the real value, and the negative correction to it should depend on the temperature. We conclude that
the contribution of all the
\bt@\alpha_{n}
whose norm diverges faster than
n
becomes asymptotically negligible compared to that coming from
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|=O(n)
.

(viii) On physical grounds we expect that the leading-order
e^{O(n)}
factor by which a cycle of length
n
contributes to the partition function is actually of order
e^{cn}
with some
c=c(\beta)>0
. Before proving this let us recall that previously we have already met terms of order
e^{o(n)}
.
A rational
\bt@\alpha
leads to
n\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]=O(1)
and at the same time the entropy is of order
\ln n
, so altogether we have a positive exponent of order
\ln n
for such terms. Terms with
\bt@\alpha
irrational but
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|=o(n)
also produce an entropy-dominated factor
e^{o(n)}>1
. Let
g\geq 1
be any monotone increasing function that tends to infinity and let
K\subset{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
be a lacunary sequence satisfying

\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\#\{k\in K|k\leq x\}}{x/g(x)}=1.

Define

\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a&{\rm if}\ j\leq j_{0},k\in K\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Then

\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|\propto i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\sim\frac{n}{g(n)},\quad\ln{n-j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\choose i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}\sim\frac{n}{g(n)}(\ln g(n)+1),

(5.52)

i.e. the entropy wins. All these terms become asymptotically independent of
\beta
and will not appear in the limiting free energy density.

To show that the leading-order contribution is due to
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|\propto n
we apply the Markov inequality to
\frac{n}{\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]
. Choose some
A>1
, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{n}{\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]<A(\lambda_{u}^{-2}-c)\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{A}.

Thus, for large enough systems

E\gtrsim\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|\left[-A\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{2}(\lambda_{u}^{-2}-c)+\frac{2}{j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}+1}\left(\ln\frac{\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1}}{a}+1-\frac{\ln 2\pi a}{2a}\right)\right]+\ln{n-j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\choose i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}}

(5.53)

with a probability not smaller than
1-1/A
. We want
E
to be positive.
A typical example for
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|\propto n
is (
5.37
); in this case
c=0
and
Y^{0}_{0}
tends to zero with probability one. However, for
\beta
large enough the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.53
) is negative, to keep it smaller in modulus than the entropy the prefactor of
n
in
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|
should decrease as
\beta
increases. Let
i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}=\epsilon n
, then the entropy, computed with
j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}=O(1)
is

\ln{n-j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\choose\epsilon n}\approx-n[\epsilon\ln\epsilon+(1-\epsilon)\ln(1-\epsilon)]=\epsilon n\ln\left[\epsilon^{-1}\left(1-\epsilon\right)^{-\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon}}\right].

(5.54)

The best chance for the exponent (
5.53
) to be positive is if
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|
is minimal under the condition that
i_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}=\epsilon n
. The minimum is attained with
\alpha^{k}_{k-1}=1
if
k\in K
where
K\subset\{2,\dots,n\}
,
|K|=\epsilon n
, and
\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=0
otherwise, resulting
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|=\epsilon n
. The exponent is then

\epsilon\left[\ln\left(\epsilon^{-1}\left(1-\epsilon\right)^{-\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon}}\right)-A\pi(\lambda_{\beta}/\lambda_{u})^{2}+\ln\frac{e\beta\|\hat{u}\|_{1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]n,

(5.55)

which is positive if

\epsilon(1-\epsilon)^{\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon}}<e^{-A\pi(\lambda_{\beta}/\lambda_{u})^{2}}.

(5.56)

Using
\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon}\ln(1-\epsilon)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\epsilon^{k}}{k(k+1)}-1
it is seen that the left side of this inequality is a monotone increasing function of
\epsilon
with the bounds

\epsilon/e\leq\epsilon(1-\epsilon)^{\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon}}\leq\epsilon.

It follows that (
5.56
) holds if
\epsilon<e^{-A\pi(\lambda_{\beta}/\lambda_{u})^{2}}
and fails if
\epsilon>e^{-A\pi(\lambda_{\beta}/\lambda_{u})^{2}+1}
.

We mentioned already an exceptional occurrence of
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|\sim n
when Eq. (
5.46
) fails, the difference (
5.45
) is
O(1/n)
while the separate terms are of order one. We return to it with the simplest example,
\alpha^{n}_{1}=1
and
\alpha^{k}_{j}=0
otherwise. Then
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|=n-1
,
{\mathbb{E}}[(Y^{0}_{0})^{2}]=\lambda_{u}^{-2}(n-1)^{2}/n^{2}
. Moreover, (keeping
\bt@\alpha^{+}=0
)
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\,\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}\,\right]=\lambda_{u}^{-2}(n-1)/n
and thus
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]=\lambda_{u}^{-2}(n-1)/n^{2}
. This is exactly the same value as the one we get if
\alpha^{k}_{k-1}=1
for a single
k\geq 2
and
\alpha^{k^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}-j^{\prime}}=0
otherwise. A similar agreement can be found between any rational sequence and a properly chosen
\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}
with a finite number of nonzero
\alpha^{k}_{k-j}
where all the
k
increase with
n
(so
\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\to\bt@0
) but all the
j
are of order 1. This is because
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|
does not depend on the value of
k
, only on the number of those for which
\alpha^{k}_{k-j}>0
.
The associated entropy is also the same as that of rational sequences, of the order of
\ln n
. Therefore either the exponent is negative or it is positive and of order
\ln n
with the conclusion that the overall contribution is asymptotically negligible.

(ix) Concerning
Y^{+}_{0}
and
\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}
, a non-negligible contribution may come from here. As a typical example,
assume that
j_{\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}}
particles of cycle 0 are coupled with
i_{\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}}
particles of cycle 1, so the number of nonzero
\alpha^{+k}_{j}
is
i_{\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}}j_{\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}}
.
Suppose also that
n_{1}
tends to infinity. The same estimations can be made as for
\bt@\alpha^{0}
. Keeping
j_{\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}}
bounded, the largest contribution is for
i_{\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}}=\epsilon n_{1}
with
\epsilon<e^{-(\pi/\gamma)(\lambda_{\beta}/\lambda_{u})^{2}}
.
However, computing the analogue of the exponent (
5.1
) for cycle 1 we find it tending to
-\infty
. The reason is that
viewed from cycle 1 the roles of
j
and
k
are interchanged,
\alpha^{+k}_{j}>0
for
\epsilon n_{1}
different
k
has the same effect on cycle 1
as
\alpha^{+k}_{j}>0
for
\epsilon n
different
j
has on cycle 0: the sum to consider,

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{1}}k\,\alpha^{+k}_{j}
diverges as
n_{1}^{2}
while the maximum entropy associated with cycle 1 can increase only as
n_{1}
.
The conclusion is that
\|\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}\|
must remain bounded.

Thus, the leading contribution of cycle 0 to
\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)
is a factor

\sim\exp\left\{ce^{-(\pi/\gamma)(\lambda_{\beta}/\lambda_{u})^{2}}n\right\}
with some
0<\gamma<1
,
coming from
\bt@\alpha=\{\bt@\alpha^{0},\bt@\alpha^{+}\}
, where
\bt@\alpha^{0}
is irrational with
\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|\propto e^{-(\pi/\gamma)(\lambda_{\beta}/\lambda_{u})^{2}}n
and
\bt@\alpha^{+}
is rational, i.e.
\|\bt@\alpha^{+}\|<\infty
. For the exponential increase in
n
the non-decay of
f_{n}(0;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}})
is also necessary. If
n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}/L^{2}
is bounded, this holds true irrespective of the value of
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
, cf. (
5.11
); if
n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}/L^{2}
tends to infinity, this is a consequence of
|\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}|=O(1/\sqrt{n})
to be shown below.

Although the role of the coupling of any individual cycle of a diverging length to the other cycles appears to be negligible, for the whole system the coupling among cycles results in a decrease of the free energy density, see the proof of Theorem
1.2
. This is because for
\beta<\infty
the partitions of
N
to
p\propto N
elements dominate the partition function: single particles and particles forming finite cycles carry the uncondensed density which is positive at positive temperatures.
It will be seen that for any
\beta
and
\rho
the dominant contribution to the partition function comes from
p\propto N
and
\bt@\alpha
chosen so that in the graph
{\cal G}_{\bt@\alpha}
a macroscopic number of cycles occur in couplings.

(x) In all the cases of
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|/n=O(1)
when
\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}
is the restriction of an infinite
\bt@\alpha^{0}
to
\{\alpha^{k}_{k-j}|1\leq j<k\leq n\}
and
\bt@\alpha^{+}
is rational
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\to 0
almost surely. To establish the rate of decay
the simplest result is obtained by using Chebyshev’s inequality: for any
A>1

{\mathbb{P}}\left(|\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}|<A\sqrt{{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]}\right)>1-1/A^{2}.

(5.57)

A bound on the standard deviation can be inferred from a comparison of Eqs. (
5.31
) and (
5.41
). Setting
\alpha^{k}_{k-j}=0
for
j>j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}
and
\alpha^{+k}_{j}=0
for
j>j_{\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}}
with
j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}},\,j_{\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}}=O(1)
justified by the previous discussion, we find

\sqrt{{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]}\leq\frac{1}{n\lambda_{u}}\sqrt{j_{\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}}\|\bt@\alpha^{0}_{n}\|+j_{\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}}\|\bt@\alpha^{+}_{n}\|}\leq\frac{c_{1}e^{-c_{2}(\lambda_{\beta}/\lambda_{u})^{2}}}{\lambda_{u}\sqrt{n}}.

(5.58)

(xi) The sign oscillation seen in
\tilde{G}\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]
is to a large extent due to the stability of the interaction. In the Fourier-representation we use here instability shows up in
\int\hat{u}(x){\,\rm d}x=u(0)<0
. In the extreme case when
\hat{u}\leq 0
all the terms of
\tilde{G}\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]
are positive. The positive-type
u
considered in this paper represents the opposite extremity:
\hat{u}\geq 0
defines a stable interaction, and the signs alternate according to the parity of the sum
\sum_{j<k}\alpha^{k}_{j}
. In the finite-volume canonical ensemble the difference between stable and unstable interactions is not striking. Stability is in the background, it guaranties the existence of the thermodynamic limit of the free energy density and, more generally, of the Gibbs measure, missing in unstable systems.
\tilde{G}\left[n,\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right]>0
, so the negative terms cannot flip the overall sign.
Because the variables occurring in terms of different signs are partly independent, exact cancellation between positive and negative terms is an event of zero probability, and therefore the order-of-magnitude estimates are not altered by them.

To summarize,
\nu^{N,L}_{n}(y)
is asymptotically concentrated to an
O(1/\sqrt{n})
neighborhood of the origin, confirming thereby the lower bound (
5.24
).
This outcome is physically satisfying: if and only if
\|\bt@\alpha_{n}\|\propto n
(and then
|\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}|\propto 1/\sqrt{n}
) the contributions of cycle 0 to the energy and the entropy are of the same order, and this is the only case when the temperature has an influence on what the cycle adds to the free energy density.

5.2
Condition for BEC

Here we do not use the asymptotic form of
f(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}})
, because the exact expressions are directly obtained:

\displaystyle f_{n}\left(0;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z+L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)^{2}\right\}

(5.59)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left\{-\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right\}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z^{2}+2z\cdot L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\right\}

and

\int_{\Lambda}f_{n}\left(x;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right){\,\rm d}x=L^{d}\exp\left\{-\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right\}=\frac{L^{d}f_{n}\left(0;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)}{\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z^{2}+2z\cdot L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\right\}}.

(5.60)

Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda}\tilde{G}\left[n_{0},\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](x){\,\rm d}x=

\displaystyle L^{d}\sum_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}|1\leq j<k\leq N\}}\ \Delta_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\},\{n_{l}\}_{0}^{p}}\left(L^{-d}\right)^{K_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\}}}\prod_{1\leq j<k\leq N}\frac{\left(-\beta\right)^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}}{\alpha^{k}_{j}!}\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}\int{\,\rm d}x^{k}_{j,r}\ \hat{u}\left(x^{k}_{j,r}\right)\int_{0}^{1}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,r}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\left[\delta(X^{0}_{1},\dots,X^{p}_{1})\prod_{l=0}^{p}\exp\left\{-\pi n_{l}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left[\overline{\left(X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right\}f_{n_{l}}\left(0;L\overline{X^{l}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left.\times\frac{1}{\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n_{0}\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z^{2}+2z\cdot L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\right\}}\right]\,.

The right-hand side without the last fraction is
L^{d}\,\tilde{G}\left[n_{0},\{n_{l}\}_{1}^{p}\right](0)
. Thus, it is the asymptotic behavior of this fraction that decides about BEC. If
n_{0}=o(N^{2/d})
, the denominator tends to infinity irrespective of the value of
\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
: if
L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}
remains bounded or increases slower than
N^{2/d}/n_{0}
then each term tends to 1; otherwise an increasing number of terms with
z\cdot L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}<0
will exceed 1. As a result, such cycles do not add to the condensate. Therefore, we focus on cycles of length
n\geq cN^{2/d}
where
c>0
.
With the definitions (
5
), (
5.14
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda}\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x){\,\rm d}x=L^{d}\sum_{\{\alpha^{k}_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}|1\leq j\leq n,\,j+1\leq k\leq N\}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{k=j+1}^{N}\frac{\left(-\beta\right)^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}}{\alpha^{k}_{j}!}\right)\int_{0}^{1}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{k=j+1}^{N}\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\,\rm d}t^{k}_{j,r}

(5.62)

\displaystyle\int\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{k=j+1}^{N}\prod_{r=1}^{\alpha^{k}_{j}}{\,\rm d}x^{k}_{j,r}\hat{u}\left(x^{k}_{j,r}\right)H_{N-n}\ \frac{\exp\left\{-\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}\left[\overline{\left(X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}\right)^{2}}-\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}^{2}\right]\right\}f_{n}\left(0;L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)}{\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z^{2}+2z\cdot L\overline{X^{0}_{{}^{\cdot}}}\right)\right\}}

\displaystyle=L^{d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\,\rm d}y\frac{\Theta^{N}_{n}(y)}{\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}\left(z^{2}+2z\cdot Ly\right)\right\}}.

Dividing with
L^{d}\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)=L^{d}\int\Theta^{N}_{n}(y^{\prime}){\,\rm d}y^{\prime}

\rho^{N,L}_{0}\geq\sum_{n\geq cN^{2/d}}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\,\frac{\int_{\Lambda}\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(x){\,\rm d}x}{L^{d}\tilde{G}^{N}_{n}(0)}=\sum_{n\geq cN^{2/d}}\rho^{N,L}_{n}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{\nu^{N,L}_{n}(y)\,{\,\rm d}y}{\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}{L^{2}}z\cdot\left(z+2Ly\right)\right\}}.

(5.63)

The result (
1.26
) for BEC follows from the fact that asymptotically
\nu^{N,L}_{n}
is concentrated to an
O(1/\sqrt{n})
neighborhood of the origin. For
n\propto N^{2/d}
this means that
L|y|=O(1)
, and if
n/N^{2/d}\to\infty
then
L|y|=o(1)
; thus, for any
n\geq cN^{2/d}
the sum over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
remains finite in the thermodynamic limit.

This ends the proof for pair potentials with
\hat{u}\geq 0
of compact support. The latter condition can be removed preserving
\int\hat{u}(x)x^{2}{\,\rm d}x<\infty
, with the only effect that the sure convergence to zero is changed into almost sure one.

6
Proof of Lemma
1.2

The lower bound is trivial except for the multipliers of
\rho
and
\rho^{2}
. It follows from superstability: there are positive constants
B
,
C
such that for
L
sufficiently large and any
N

\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq N}u_{L}(x_{k}-x_{j})\geq-BN+CN(N-1)/L^{d}.

The largest
C
is

C_{\Lambda}[u]=\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{L^{d}}{N(N-1)}\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq N}u_{L}(x_{k}-x_{j})

(6.1)

that we called in [Su5] the best superstability constant (the Fekete constant in potential theory [Ch]). In general
C_{\Lambda}[u]\leq\hat{u}(0)/2
, but in Section 9 of [Su5] it was shown that for
u
of the positive type
C_{\Lambda}[u]=\hat{u}(0)/2
independently of
L
.
Even if
u\geq 0
,
B>0
must be chosen, otherwise the inequality could fail for small
N
, e.g. if
u
is of finite range. Also, for
N
large the quadratic term alone with
C=C_{\Lambda}[u]
can be too large. If
u
is bounded,
C=C_{\Lambda}[u]
and
B=u_{L}(0)/2
provide a valid lower bound.

For any partition of
N

\sum_{l=0}^{p}U(\omega_{l})+\sum_{0\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq p}U(\omega_{l^{\prime}},\omega_{l})\geq-BN+C_{\Lambda}[u]N(N-1)/L^{d},

hence

\displaystyle Q_{N,L}\leq e^{-\beta\left[-BN+C_{\Lambda}[u]N(N-1)/L^{d}\right]}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\int W^{n\beta}_{xx}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})

\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{N-n}\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\geq 1:\sum n_{l}=N-n}\prod_{l=1}^{p}\frac{1}{n_{l}}\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x_{l}\int W^{n_{l}\beta}_{x_{l}x_{l}}({\,\rm d}\omega_{l})=e^{-\beta\left[-BN+C_{\Lambda}[u]N(N-1)/L^{d}\right]}Q^{0}_{N,L}

from which the lower bound for
f(\rho,\beta)
follows with
u_{L}\to u
as
L\to\infty
and

C[u]=\liminf_{L\to\infty}C_{\Lambda}[u].

(6.2)

The upper bound in (
1.43
) and (
1.44
) is based on Jensen’s inequality. First, on the torus
Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0}+y)=Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0})
, because any shift of
\omega_{0}
can be defused by the same shift of the integration variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{p}
and hence of
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{p}
; therefore

Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0})=\frac{1}{L^{d}}\int_{\Lambda}Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0}+y){\,\rm d}y.

The dependence on
\omega_{0}
is only in the last exponential factor of
Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0})
. Applying Jensen’s inequality and

\int_{\Lambda}u_{L}(y){\,\rm d}y=\int u(y){\,\rm d}y\leq\|u\|_{1},

\frac{1}{L^{d}}\int_{\Lambda}e^{-\beta\sum_{l=1}^{p}U(\omega_{0}+y,\omega_{l})}{\,\rm d}y\geq e^{-\beta\frac{1}{L^{d}}\int_{\Lambda}\sum_{l=1}^{p}U(\omega_{0}+y,\omega_{l}){\,\rm d}y}=e^{-\beta\frac{n(N-n)}{L^{d}}\int_{\Lambda}u_{L}(y){\,\rm d}y}\geq e^{-\beta\frac{n(N-n)}{L^{d}}\|u\|_{1}}.

Thus, for any
\omega_{0}

Q_{N-n,L}(\omega_{0})\geq e^{-\beta\frac{n(N-n)}{L^{d}}\|u\|_{1}}Q_{N-n,L}

(6.3)

and consequently

Q_{N,L}\geq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\exp\left\{-\beta\|u\|_{1}\frac{n(N-n)}{L^{d}}\right\}Q_{N-n,L}\,\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\int W^{n\beta}_{xx}({\,\rm d}\omega_{0})e^{-\beta U(\omega_{0})}.

(6.4)

Recall from Eqs. (
2.12
)-(
2.14
) the elementary properties of the conditional Wiener measures
P^{\beta}_{xy}({\,\rm d}\omega)
and
W^{\beta}_{xy}({\,\rm d}\omega)
. We will supplement them with two more.
Let

\psi_{t}(x)=\lambda_{t}^{-d}e^{-\pi x^{2}/\lambda_{t}^{2}},

then

\displaystyle\int P^{\beta}_{xy}({\,\rm d}\omega)=\psi_{\beta}(y-x).

(6.5)

Throughout the proof we use the following identity.
If
f(\omega)=f(\omega(t_{0}))
with
0<t_{0}<\beta
, then

\int P^{\beta}_{xy}({\,\rm d}\omega)f(\omega)=\int{\,\rm d}x^{\prime}\psi_{t_{0}}(x-x^{\prime})f(x^{\prime})\psi_{\beta-t_{0}}(x^{\prime}-y).

(6.6)

By Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda}{\,\rm d}x\int W^{n\beta}_{xx}({\,\rm d}\omega)e^{-\beta U(\omega)}=L^{d}\int W^{n\beta}_{00}({\,\rm d}\omega)e^{-\beta U(\omega)}=L^{d}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\int P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)e^{-\beta U(\omega)}

\displaystyle\geq L^{d}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\left(\int P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)\right)\exp\left\{-\beta\frac{\int P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)U(\omega)}{\int P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{L^{d}}{n^{d/2}\lambda_{\beta}^{d}}\sum_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi L^{2}z^{2}}{n\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}\right\}\exp\left\{-\beta\langle U\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}\right\}.

(6.7)

We turn to
\langle U\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}
.
With a slight extension of (
6.6
)
one can show that equal-time increments have the same distribution. Let
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\beta
,
\omega(0)=x
,
\omega(\beta)=y
, and consider any
f
depending only on
\omega(t_{2})-\omega(t_{1})
. Then

\displaystyle\int P^{\beta}_{xy}({\,\rm d}\omega)f(\omega(t_{2})-\omega(t_{1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\,\rm d}x_{1}{\,\rm d}x_{2}\ \psi_{t_{1}}(x_{1}-x)\psi_{t_{2}-t_{1}}(x_{2}-x_{1})\psi_{\beta-t_{2}}(y-x_{2})f(x_{2}-x_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\,\rm d}z\ \psi_{t_{2}-t_{1}}(z)f(z)\int{\,\rm d}x_{1}\ \psi_{t_{1}}(x_{1}-x)\psi_{\beta-t_{2}}(y-z-x_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\,\rm d}z\ \psi_{t_{2}-t_{1}}(z)f(z)\psi_{\beta-(t_{2}-t_{1})}(y-x-z)=\int P^{\beta}_{0,y-x}({\,\rm d}\omega)f(\omega(t_{2}-t_{1})).

Thus,

\int P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)u_{L}(\omega(k\beta+t)-\omega(j\beta+t))=\int P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)u_{L}(\omega((k-j)\beta))

(6.9)

and

\langle U\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}=\sum_{0\leq j<k\leq n-1}\langle u_{L}(\omega((k-j)\beta))\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(n-k)\langle u_{L}(\omega(k\beta))\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}k\langle u_{L}(\omega((n-k)\beta))\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}.

(6.10)

For any
z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
,

u_{L}(x)=u_{L}(Lz-x),

from which with (
6.6
) one obtains

\int P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)u_{L}(\omega((n-k)\beta))=\int P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)u_{L}(\omega(k\beta)).

(6.11)

Summing the two forms (
6.10
) of
\langle U\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}
and using the above equality,

\langle U\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}=\frac{n}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\langle u_{L}(\omega(k\beta))\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}.

(6.12)

Next,

\displaystyle\langle u_{L}(\omega(k\beta))\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)\right)^{-1}\int P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}({\,\rm d}\omega)u_{L}(\omega(k\beta))

(6.13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{n\beta}(Lz)^{-1}\int\psi_{k\beta}(x)\psi_{(n-k)\beta}(Lz-x)u_{L}(x){\,\rm d}x.

A straightforward calculation yields

\psi_{n\beta}(Lz)^{-1}\psi_{k\beta}(x)\psi_{(n-k)\beta}(Lz-x)=\alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}\exp\{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}(x-Lkz/n)^{2}\}

(6.14)

and therefore

\langle u_{L}(\omega(k\beta))\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}=\alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}\int u_{L}(x)\exp\left\{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}(x-Lkz/n)^{2}\right\}{\,\rm d}x

(6.15)

where

\alpha_{n,k}=\left(\frac{1}{k}+\frac{1}{n-k}\right)\frac{1}{\lambda_{\beta}^{2}}.

(6.16)

Substituting
u_{L}(x)=\sum_{v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}u(x-Lv)
into (
6.15
),

\displaystyle\langle u_{L}(\omega(k\beta))\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}\sum_{v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\int u(x-Lv)\exp\left\{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}(x-Lkz/n)^{2}\right\}{\,\rm d}x

(6.17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int u(y)\ \alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}\sum_{v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}(y-L\{kz/n\}+Lv)^{2}\right\}{\,\rm d}y

where
\{kz/n\}
is the fractional part of
kz/n
for
z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
, each component of which is bounded in modulus by
1/2
.
Applying twice the Poisson summation formula

\displaystyle\alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}\sum_{v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}(y-L\{kz/n\}+Lv)^{2}\right\}=L^{-d}\sum_{v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi v^{2}}{\alpha_{n,k}L^{2}}+{\,\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{L}(y-L\{kz/n\})\cdot v\right\}

\displaystyle\leq L^{-d}\sum_{v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi v^{2}}{\alpha_{n,k}L^{2}}\right\}=\alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}\sum_{v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}L^{2}v^{2}\right\}.

(6.18)

So

\langle u_{L}(\omega(k\beta))\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}\leq\|u\|_{1}\alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}\sum_{v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}L^{2}v^{2}\right\},

(6.19)

the
z
-dependence dropped from the upper bound.
Now

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}e^{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}L^{2}v^{2}}=\left(1+2\sum_{v=1}^{\infty}e^{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}L^{2}v^{2}}\right)^{d}\leq\left(1+2\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}L^{2}v^{2}}{\,\rm d}v\right)^{d}=\left(1+\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\alpha_{n,k}}}\right)^{d}.

(6.20)

Inserting this into the expression for
\langle U\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}
,

\displaystyle\langle U\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{n}{2}\|u\|_{1}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}\sum_{v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-\pi\alpha_{n,k}L^{2}v^{2}\right\}\leq\frac{n}{2}\|u\|_{1}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}\left(1+\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\alpha_{n,k}}}\right)^{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n}{2}\|u\|_{1}\sum_{l=0}^{d}{d\choose l}\frac{1}{L^{d-l}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\alpha_{n,k}^{l/2}=\frac{n}{2}\|u\|_{1}\left[\frac{n-1}{L^{d}}+\sum_{l=1}^{d-1}{d\choose l}\frac{1}{L^{d-l}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\alpha_{n,k}^{l/2}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}\right].

For
d\geq 3
and
1\leq l\leq d-1

\frac{1}{L^{d-l}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\alpha_{n,k}^{l/2}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\beta}^{l}L^{d-l}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{k}+\frac{1}{n-k}\right)^{l/2}\leq\frac{2^{l/2}}{\lambda_{\beta}^{l}L^{d-l}}\sum_{k=1}^{n/2}\frac{1}{k^{l/2}}=o(1)\quad(L\to\infty)

(6.22)

which finally yields

\langle U\rangle_{P^{n\beta}_{0,Lz}}\leq\frac{\|u\|_{1}}{2}n\left[\frac{n-1}{L^{d}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\alpha_{n,k}^{d/2}+o(1)\right]\leq\frac{\|u\|_{1}}{2}n\left[\frac{n-1}{L^{d}}+2^{d/2}\zeta(d/2)/\lambda_{\beta}^{d}+o(1)\right].

(6.23)

Dropping the
o(1)
term,
the inequality (
6.4
) can be continued as

\displaystyle Q_{N,L}

\displaystyle\geq
